\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\quad\phantom{+}h_{\infty,12},h_{\infty,23};{\bf h}^{c_{1};2}_{1},{\bf h}_{2},{\bf h}^{c_{3};2}_{3}\bigr);{\bf h}^{c_{3};1}_{3}\bigr)=0.

(9.25)

Moreover,
\mathscr{YT}\equiv\mathscr{YT}^{<E_{0},\varepsilon}\mod T^{E_{0}}
.

We call
\mathscr{YT}
, the
Y diagram transformation
also.

9.3
Proof of Proposition
9.2
(2)

In this subsection, we prove Proposition
9.2
(2).

Let
\mathcal{L}_{12}=(L_{12},\sigma_{12},b_{12})

(resp.
\mathcal{L}_{23}=(L_{23},\sigma_{23},b_{23})
)
be an object of
\mathfrak{Fukst}(-X_{1}\times X_{2})

(resp.
{\mathfrak{Fukst}(-X_{2}\times X_{3})}
).
Let
\mathcal{L}_{13}=(L_{13},\sigma_{13},b_{13})

be the geometric composition

\mathcal{L}_{23}\circ\mathcal{L}_{12}
.

Let
\mathcal{L}_{1}=(L_{1},\sigma_{1},b_{1})

and we put

\mathcal{L}_{2}=(L_{2},\sigma_{2},b_{2})=\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{L}_{12}}(\mathcal{L}_{1}),\quad\mathcal{L}_{3}^{(1)}=\bigl(L_{3},\sigma_{3},b^{(1)}_{3}\bigr)=\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{L}_{23}}(\mathcal{L}_{2})

and

\mathcal{L}_{3}^{(2)}=\bigl(L_{3},\sigma_{3},b^{(2)}_{3}\bigr)=\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{L}_{13}}(\mathcal{L}_{1}).

We remark that the underlying Lagrangian submanifold of

\mathcal{L}_{3}^{(1)}
is equal to the underlying Lagrangian submanifold
of
\mathcal{L}_{3}^{(2)}
.
This is obvious since

L_{2}\times_{X_{2}}L_{23}=L_{1}\times_{X_{1}}L_{12}\times_{X_{2}}L_{23}=L_{1}\times_{X_{1}}L_{13}.

The coincidence of the relative spin structure is the main part of Proposition
9.2
(1)
which we will prove in Section
.
We will prove in this subsection the next proposition.

Proposition 9.13
.

The bounding cochain

b^{(1)}_{3}
is gauge equivalent to
b^{(2)}_{3}
in the sense of

[
fooobook
,
Definition
4.3.1]
.

Proof.

We use the next algebraic lemma to prove Proposition
9.13
.

Lemma 9.14
.

Let
(D,\{\mathfrak{n}_{k}\})
be a
G
-gapped right filtered
A_{\infty}
module over

(C,\{\mathfrak{m}_{k}\})
.
Let
{\bf 1}^{(1)}
,
{\bf 1}^{(2)}
be cyclic elements of
D

and
b^{(1)}
,
b^{(2)}
bounding cochains of
C
such that

\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\mathfrak{n}_{k}\bigl({\bf 1}^{(i)};b^{(i)},\dots,b^{(i)}\bigr)=0.

We also assume

{\bf 1}^{(1)}\equiv{\bf 1}^{(2)}\mod\Lambda_{+}.

(9.26)

Then
b^{(1)}
is gauge equivalent to
b^{(2)}
.

Proof.

We use a certain result and notations of
[
fooobook
]
in the proof.
Let
\mathfrak{C}
be a model of
[0,1]\times C
in the sense of
[
fooobook
, Definition 4.2.1]
.
Let
\mathfrak{D}
be a model of
[0,1]\times D
in the sense of
[
fooobook
, Definition 5.2.21]
,
which is a right
\mathfrak{C}
module.
Such
\mathfrak{C}
and
\mathfrak{D}
exists by
[
fooobook
, Lemma 4.2.13 and Theorem 5.2.23]
.
Since
{\rm Eval}_{0}\oplus{\rm Eval}_{1}\colon\mathfrak{D}\to D\oplus D

is surjective (see
[
fooobook
, Definition 5.2.23]
),
we have

\Delta{\bf 1}\in\mathfrak{D}
such that

({\rm Eval}_{0})(\Delta{\bf 1})=0
,

({\rm Eval}_{1})(\Delta{\bf 1})={\bf 1}^{(2)}-{\bf 1}^{(1)}
.
Using (
9.26
), we may choose
\Delta{\bf 1}
such that

\Delta{\bf 1}\equiv 0\mod\Lambda_{+}.

(9.27)

We put

\hat{\bf 1}={\rm Incl}({\bf 1}^{(1)})+\Delta{\bf 1}
.
(
9.27
) implies that
\hat{\bf 1}
is a cyclic element of the
right
\mathfrak{C}
module
\mathfrak{D}
.
Therefore, by Proposition
6.6
there exists a bounding cochain
\hat{b}
of
\mathfrak{c}
such that

\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\mathfrak{n}_{k}\bigl(\hat{\bf 1};\hat{b},\dots,\hat{b}\bigr)=0.

We remark that

({\rm Eval}_{0})\bigl(\hat{\bf 1}\bigr)={\bf 1}^{(1)}
,
({\rm Eval}_{1})\bigl(\hat{\bf 1}\bigr)={\bf 1}^{(2)}
.
Therefore, using the uniqueness part of Proposition
6.6

we find that

({\rm Eval}_{0})\bigl(\hat{b}\bigr)=b^{(1)}
,
({\rm Eval}_{1})\bigl(\hat{b}\bigr)=b^{(2)}
.
Hence
b^{(1)}
is gauge equivalent to
b^{(2)}
, as required.
∎

We go back to our geometric situation and use Y diagram transformation

\mathscr{YT}
to
define a map

\mathscr{MY}\colon\ CF(L_{1};L_{12};L_{2})\otimes CF(L_{2};L_{23};L_{3})\to CF(L_{1};L_{13};L_{3})

(9.28)

by

\mathscr{MY}(h_{\infty,+,12},h_{\infty,+,23})=\mathscr{YT}\bigl({\bf 1}_{123};e^{b_{12}},e^{b_{23}},e^{b_{13}};h_{\infty,+,12},h_{\infty,+,23};e^{b_{1}},e^{b_{3}},e^{b_{3}^{(1)}}\bigr).

Here
{\bf 1}_{123}\in CF(L_{12};L_{23};L_{13})
is the cyclic element we used in Lemma
8.10
.
(See Figure
9.8
.)
In other words, it is the function
1
on the diagonal component of

\bigl(\tilde{L}_{12}\times\tilde{L}_{23}\times\tilde{L}_{13}\bigr)\times_{(X_{1}\times X_{2}\times X_{3})^{2}}\Delta\cong\tilde{L}_{13}\times_{X_{1}\times X_{3}}\tilde{L}_{13}.

Figure 9.8:
The map
\mathscr{MY}
.

Note that
CF(L_{i},L_{ii^{\prime}};L_{i^{\prime}})
for
ii^{\prime}=12,23
or
13
is a filtered
A_{\infty}

tri-module over
CF(L_{i})
,
CF(L_{ii^{\prime}})
,
CF(L_{i^{\prime}})
.
Therefore, bounding cochains of
CF(L_{i})
,
CF(L_{ii^{\prime}})
,
CF(L_{i^{\prime}})

deform their ‘boundary operators’ to obtain a boundary operator.
Namely, if
b_{i}b_{ii^{\prime}}
,
b_{i^{\prime}}
are bounding cochains of

CF(L_{i})
,
CF(L_{ii^{\prime}})
,
CF(L_{i^{\prime}})
, we put

d^{b_{i};b_{ii^{\prime}};b_{i^{\prime}}}(x)=\mathfrak{n}\bigl(e^{b_{i}},e^{b_{ii^{\prime}}};x;e^{b_{i^{\prime}}}\bigr)
,
where
\mathfrak{n}
is the structure operations of the tri-module
in Theorem
5.25
.

Lemma 9.15
.

The map
\mathscr{MY}
in (
9.28
) is a chain map
with respect to the boundary operators
d^{b_{1};b_{12};b_{2}}
,
d^{b_{2};b_{23};b^{(1)}_{3}}
,
d^{b_{1};b_{13};b^{(1)}_{3}}
.

Proof.

We put
h_{\infty,123}={\bf 1}_{123}
,
{\bf h}_{12}=e^{b_{12}}
,
{\bf h}_{23}=e^{b_{23}}
,
{\bf h}_{13}=e^{b_{13}}
,

{\bf h}_{1}=e^{b_{1}}
,
{\bf h}_{2}=e^{b_{2}}
,
{\bf h}_{3}=e^{b^{(1)}_{3}}

and apply Proposition
9.12
.
The first 6 terms of (
9.24
) vanish because
{\bf h}_{ii^{\prime}}
,
{\bf h}_{i}
are exponentials
of the bounding cochains. The 7th term vanishes because
{\bf 1}_{123}
is a cycle
with respect to the differential of
{CF}(L_{13};L_{12},L_{23})
twisted by

b_{12}
,
b_{23}
,
b_{13}
.
In fact, this is the definition of
b_{13}
. (See (
8.4
).)
The 8th, 9th, 10th terms give the elements

\mathscr{YT}(d^{b_{1};b_{12};b_{2}}(h_{\infty,12}),h_{\infty,23})
,

\mathscr{YT}(h_{\infty,12},d^{b_{1};b_{23};b^{(1)}_{3}}(h_{\infty,23}))
,
and

d^{b_{1};b_{13};b^{(1)}_{3}}(\mathscr{YT}(h_{\infty,12},h_{\infty,23}))

respectively.
The lemma follows.
∎

For
ii^{\prime}=12,23
or
13
, we denote by

{\bf 1}_{ii^{\prime}}
the function
1
on the diagonal component of

\tilde{L}_{i}\times_{X_{i}\times X_{i^{\prime}}}L_{i^{\prime}}
.

Lemma 9.16
.

\mathscr{YT}({\bf 1}_{12},{\bf 1}_{23})\equiv{\bf 1}_{13}\mod\Lambda_{+}
.

Proof.

The operation
\mathscr{YT}
are defined modulo
\Lambda_{+}
by the integration along the
fiber of the moduli space

{\mathcal{M}}_{\rm Y}(\vec{a}_{12},\vec{a}_{23},\vec{a}_{13};\vec{a}_{1},\vec{a}_{2},\vec{a}_{3},a_{\infty,123},\vec{a}_{\infty};0)
,
which consists of constant maps.
Using this fact and the definitions, we can prove the lemma easily in the same way as
Proposition
6.12
.
∎

We recall that on
CF(L_{1},L_{13};L_{3})
we have a structure of
right
CF(L_{3})
module
\mathfrak{n}_{k}
. In fact, we put

\mathfrak{n}_{k}(y;x_{1},\dots,x_{k})=\mathfrak{n}\bigl(e^{b_{1}};e^{b_{13}};y;x_{1},\dots,x_{k}\bigr)

(see Lemma
6.10
).

By the definition of
b_{3}^{(2)}
, we have

\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\mathfrak{n}_{k}\bigl({\bf 1}_{13};b_{3}^{(2)},\dots,b_{3}^{(2)}\bigr)=0.

(9.29)

We put

{\bf 1}^{\prime}_{13}=\mathscr{YT}({\bf 1}_{12},{\bf 1}_{23})
.
Then by Lemma
9.15
, we have

\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\mathfrak{n}_{k}\bigl({\bf 1}^{\prime}_{13};b_{3}^{(1)},\dots,b_{3}^{(1)}\bigr)=0.

(9.30)

By (
9.29
), (
9.30
) and Lemma
9.16
, we can apply Lemma
9.14

to conclude that
b_{3}^{(1)}
is gauge equivalent to
b_{3}^{(2)}
.
The proof of Proposition
9.2
(2) is complete.
∎

10
The compatibility as 2-functors

10.1
The composition of
\boldsymbol{A_{\infty}}
functors defines a bi-functor

To obtain a more functorial version of Theorem
9.1
,
we need the following algebraic result.

Theorem 10.1
.

Let
\mathscr{C}_{i}
be a unital, strict and gapped filtered
A_{\infty}
category for

i=1,2,3
.
Then, there exists a filtered
A_{\infty}
bi-functor

\mathfrak{Comp}\colon\ \mathcal{FUNC}(\mathscr{C}_{1},\mathscr{C}_{2})\times\mathcal{FUNC}(\mathscr{C}_{2},\mathscr{C}_{3})\to\mathcal{FUNC}(\mathscr{C}_{1},\mathscr{C}_{3})

(10.1)

such that

\mathfrak{Comp}_{\rm ob}(\mathscr{F}_{12},\mathscr{F}_{23})=\mathscr{F}_{23}\circ\mathscr{F}_{12}
.

We fix a discrete monoid
G\subset{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}
. Here and hereafter the objects

\mathcal{FUNC}(\mathscr{C}_{1},\mathscr{C}_{2})

are strict, unital and
G
-gapped filtered
A_{\infty}
functors.

Remark 10.2
.

Theorem
10.1
could be a part of the construction of an
(
A_{\infty}
) 2-category whose object is a
filtered
A_{\infty}
category. See Section
10.6
.

The unfiltered version of this statement is in
[
Ly
]
.
We prove it here since we need the construction of
the functor
\mathfrak{Comp}
for our application to geometry
in Sections
10.2
and
10.5
.
Our proof below is different from the proof in
[
Ly
]
.

Proof.

Let
\mathscr{C}_{1}
,
\mathscr{C}_{2}
be unital, strict and gapped
filtered
A_{\infty}
categories.

Lemma–Definition 10.3
.

There exists a filtered
A_{\infty}
functor

\mathfrak{RYon}\colon\ \mathcal{FUNC}(\mathscr{C}_{1},\mathscr{C}_{2})\to\mathcal{BIMOD}(\mathscr{C}_{1},\mathscr{C}_{2})^{\rm op},

which is a homotopy equivalence to its image.
We call this functor
the
relative Yoneda functor
.

Proof.

The functor
\mathfrak{OpYon}
(for
\mathscr{C}_{2}
)
and the isomorphism in Lemma
2.33
induces

\mathcal{FUNC}(\mathscr{C}_{1},\mathscr{C}_{2})\cong\mathcal{FUNC}(\mathscr{C}_{1}^{\rm op},\mathscr{C}_{2}^{\rm op})^{\rm op}\to\mathcal{FUNC}(\mathscr{C}^{\rm op}_{1},\mathcal{FUNC}(\mathscr{C}_{2},\mathcal{CH}))^{\rm op}.

On the other hand, in Definition
5.14
, we defined an isomorphism

\mathcal{FUNC}(\mathscr{C}^{\rm op}_{1},\mathcal{FUNC}(\mathscr{C}_{2},\mathcal{CH}))\cong\mathcal{BIMOD}(\mathscr{C}_{1};\mathscr{C}_{2}).

The lemma follows.
∎

Let us describe the functor
\mathfrak{RYon}
more explicitly below.
Let
\mathfrak{F}\colon\mathscr{C}_{1}\to\mathscr{C}_{2}
be an object of
\mathcal{FUNC}(\mathscr{C}_{1},\mathscr{C}_{2})
.
We first define left-
\mathscr{C}_{1}
and right-
\mathscr{C}_{2}
bi-module

\mathfrak{RYon}_{\rm ob}(\mathfrak{F})
.
Let
c_{i}
be an object of
\mathscr{C}_{i}
for
i=1,2
.
We put

D_{c_{1},c_{2}}=\mathscr{C}_{2}(\mathfrak{F}_{\rm ob}(c_{1}),c_{2})
.
Let
{\bf x}\in B\mathscr{C}_{1}(c^{\prime}_{1},c_{1})
,

z\in D_{c_{1},c_{2}}
,

{\bf y}\in B\mathscr{C}_{2}(c_{2},c^{\prime}_{2})
.
We define

\mathfrak{n}({\bf x},z,{\bf y})\in D_{c^{\prime}_{1},c^{\prime}_{2}}=\mathscr{C}_{2}(\mathfrak{F}_{\rm ob}(c^{\prime}_{1}),c^{\prime}_{2})

by

\mathfrak{n}({\bf x},z,{\bf y})=(-1)^{\deg^{\prime}{\bf y}}\mathfrak{m}\bigl(\widehat{\mathfrak{F}}({\bf x}),z,{\bf y}\bigr).

(10.2)

Remark 10.4
.

In the definition of relative Yoneda functor, we use

\mathfrak{OpYon}
which is defined by using opposite category

\mathscr{C}^{\rm op}
. Moreover, we use the isomorphism

\mathcal{FUNC}(\mathscr{C}_{1},\mathscr{C}_{2})\cong\mathcal{FUNC}(\mathscr{C}_{1}^{\rm op},\allowbreak\mathscr{C}_{2}^{\rm op})^{\rm op}
.
Since we take the operation taking opposite twice

{\bf x}
in the left-hand side becomes
{\bf x}
in the right hand
side.
The
+1
in Definition
2.30
(3) cancels with the minus
sign in Definition
2.18
.

A rather complicated process to define
\mathfrak{OpYon}

becomes a simple and natural formula (
10.2
),
when we write it explicitly.

The languages of functors and of bi-modules are mostly equivalent
when the target is
\mathcal{CH}
. However, the identification
includes the process taking opposite.

We will check (
5.12
).
Let

\Delta{\bf x}=\sum_{a_{1}}\text{\bf x}_{a_{1};1}\otimes\text{\bf x}_{a_{1};2}
,

\Delta{\bf y}=\sum_{a_{2}}\text{\bf y}_{a_{2};1}\otimes\text{\bf y}_{a_{2};2}
.
By definition, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{a_{1}}\sum_{a_{2}}(-1)^{*_{2}}\mathfrak{n}(\text{\bf x}_{a_{1};1},\mathfrak{n}(\text{\bf x}_{a_{1};2},z,\text{\bf y}_{a_{2};1}),\text{\bf y}_{a_{2};2})

\displaystyle\qquad=\sum_{a_{1}}\sum_{a_{2}}(-1)^{*_{3}}\mathfrak{m}\bigl(\widehat{\mathfrak{F}}({\bf x}_{a_{1};1}),\mathfrak{m}(\widehat{\mathfrak{F}}({\bf x}_{a_{2};1}),z,\text{\bf y}_{a_{2};1}),\text{\bf y}_{a_{2};2}\bigr),

(10.3)

where
*_{2}=\deg^{\prime}\text{\bf x}_{a_{1};1}

and
*_{3}=\deg^{\prime}{\bf x}_{a_{2};1}+\deg^{\prime}{\bf y}
.

Moreover,

\mathfrak{n}\bigl(\hat{d}(\text{\bf x}),z,\text{\bf y}\bigr)=(-1)^{\deg^{\prime}{\bf y}}\mathfrak{m}\bigl(\widehat{\mathfrak{F}}(\hat{d}{\bf x}),z,{\bf y}\bigr)=(-1)^{\deg^{\prime}{\bf y}}\mathfrak{m}\bigl(\hat{d}(\widehat{\mathfrak{F}}({\bf x})),z,{\bf y}\bigr).

(10.4)

Here the second equality follows from the fact that
\mathfrak{F}
is a filtered
A_{\infty}
functor.
Furthermore,

(-1)^{\deg^{\prime}{\bf x}+\deg z}\mathfrak{n}\bigl(\text{\bf x},z,\hat{d}(\text{\bf y})\bigr)=\sum_{a_{1}}(-1)^{*_{4}}\mathfrak{m}\bigl(\widehat{\mathfrak{F}}({\bf x}),z,\hat{d}({\bf y})\bigr),

(10.5)

where
*_{4}=\deg^{\prime}{\bf x}+\deg z+\deg^{\prime}{\bf y}
.

Formulas (
10.3
), (
10.4
), (
10.5
) and
the
A_{\infty}
formula for

\mathfrak{m}
imply (
5.12
) with sign modified (see Remark
10.5
), using the fact that
\widehat{\mathfrak{F}}
is a cohomomorphism.
Thus
D_{c_{1},c_{2}}
equipped with this bi-module structure is

\mathfrak{RYon}_{\rm ob}(\mathcal{F})(c_{1},c_{2})
.

Remark 10.5
.

In this and the next sections, we use the sign convention of filtered
A_{\infty}

modules (multi-modules) so that the degree of elements of modules are not
shifted.
In other words, in (
10.5
),
\deg z
appears in place of
\deg^{\prime}z
. The sign
(-1)^{\deg^{\prime}{\bf y}}
in (
10.2
)
appears by this reason.
See Remark
5.5
.

A natural transformation
\mathcal{T}

from
\mathfrak{F}
to
\mathfrak{G}
gives

\mathcal{T}_{0}(c_{1})\in\mathscr{C}_{2}(\mathfrak{F}_{\rm ob}(c_{1}),\mathfrak{G}_{\rm ob}(c_{1}))
.
It induces a cochain map

\mathscr{C}_{2}(\mathfrak{G}_{\rm ob}(c_{1}),c_{2})\to\mathscr{C}_{2}(\mathfrak{F}_{\rm ob}(c_{1}),c_{2}).

This is a part of a bi-module homomorphism
from
\mathfrak{RYon}_{\rm ob}(\mathfrak{G})
to
\mathfrak{RYon}_{\rm ob}(\mathfrak{F})
.
The direction of the arrows are opposite. This is the reason why
the opposite category appears in Lemma–Definition
10.3
.

The next lemma-definition, Propositions
10.10
and
10.23
are
closely related to the work
[
To
]
by Toën.

Lemma–Definition 10.6
.

Let
\mathscr{C}_{1}
,
\mathscr{C}_{2}
,
\mathscr{C}_{3}
be filtered
A_{\infty}

categories.
There exists a filtered
A_{\infty}
bi-functor

\mathfrak{ten}\colon\ \mathcal{BIMOD}(\mathscr{C}_{1},\mathscr{C}_{2})\times\mathcal{BIMOD}(\mathscr{C}_{2},\mathscr{C}_{3})\to\mathcal{BIMOD}(\mathscr{C}_{1},\mathscr{C}_{3}).

We call it the derived tensor product functor.

Proof.

Let

\mathfrak{D}^{12}=\bigl(\bigl\{D^{12}_{c_{1},c_{2}}\bigr\},\bigl\{\mathfrak{n}^{12}_{c_{1}^{\prime},c_{1},c_{2},c_{2}^{\prime}}\bigr\}\bigr)

be an object of

\mathcal{BIMOD}(\mathscr{C}_{1},\mathscr{C}_{2})

and let

\mathfrak{D}^{23}=\bigl(\bigl\{D^{23}_{c_{2},c_{3}}\bigr\},\bigl\{\mathfrak{n}^{23}_{c_{2}^{\prime},c_{2},c_{3},c_{3}^{\prime}}\bigr\}\bigr)

be an object of

\mathcal{BIMOD}(\mathscr{C}_{1},\mathscr{C}_{2})
.
We will define an object

\mathfrak{D}^{13}=\bigl(\bigl\{D^{13}_{c_{1},c_{3}}\bigr\},\bigl\{\mathfrak{n}^{13}_{c_{1}^{\prime},c_{1},c_{3},c_{3}^{\prime}}\bigr\}\bigr)

of
\mathcal{BIMOD}(\mathscr{C}_{1},\mathscr{C}_{3})
.

Let
c_{1},c^{\prime}_{1}\in\mathfrak{OB}(\mathscr{C}_{1})
,

c_{3},c^{\prime}_{3}\in\mathfrak{OB}(\mathscr{C}_{3})
.
We put

D^{13}_{c_{1},c_{3}}=\underset{c_{2},c^{\prime}_{2}}{\widehat{\bigoplus}}D^{12}_{c_{1},c_{2}}\,\widehat{\otimes}\,B\mathscr{C}_{2}[1](c_{2},c^{\prime}_{2})\,\widehat{\otimes}\,D^{23}_{c^{\prime}_{2},c_{3}}.

(10.6)

We remark that
B\mathscr{C}_{2}[1](c_{2},c^{\prime}_{2})

contains
1\in B_{0}\mathscr{C}_{2}[1](c_{2},c_{2})\cong\Lambda_{0}

when
c_{2}=c^{\prime}_{2}
.

Let
{\bf x}\in B\mathscr{C}_{1}[1](c^{\prime}_{1},c_{1})
,

{\bf y}\in B\mathscr{C}_{3}[1](c_{3},c^{\prime}_{3})

and

z=u\otimes{\bf v}\otimes w\in D^{12}_{c_{1},c_{2}}\,\widehat{\otimes}\,B\mathscr{C}_{2}[1](c_{2},c^{\prime}_{2})\,\widehat{\otimes}\,D^{23}_{c^{\prime}_{2},c_{3}}\subseteq D^{13}_{c_{1},c_{3}}.

We define

\mathfrak{n}^{13}_{c_{1}^{\prime},c_{1},c_{3},c_{3}^{\prime}}\colon B\mathscr{C}_{1}[1](c^{\prime}_{1},c_{1})\,\widehat{\otimes}\,D^{13}_{c_{1},c_{3}}\otimes B\mathscr{C}_{3}[1](c_{3},c^{\prime}_{3})\to D^{13}_{c^{\prime}_{1},c^{\prime}_{3}}

by

\displaystyle\mathfrak{n}^{13}_{c_{1}^{\prime},c_{1},c_{3},c_{3}^{\prime}}({\bf x},z,{\bf y}):=\begin{cases}\sum_{a}\mathfrak{n}^{12}({\bf x},u,{\bf v}_{a;1})\otimes{\bf v}_{a;2}\otimes w&\text{if ${\bf y}=1\in B_{0}\mathscr{C}_{3}(c_{3},c^{\prime}_{3})$},\\
\sum_{a}(-1)^{*}u\otimes{\bf v}_{1:a}\otimes\mathfrak{n}^{23}({\bf v}_{a;2},w,{\bf y})&\text{if ${\bf x}=1\in B_{0}\mathscr{C}_{1}(c_{1},c^{\prime}_{1})$},\\
\sum_{a}\mathfrak{n}^{12}(u,{\bf v}_{a;1})\otimes{\bf v}_{a;2}&\\
\quad+\sum_{a}(-1)^{*}u\otimes{\bf v}_{a;1}\otimes\mathfrak{n}^{23}({\bf v}_{a;2},w)&\\
\quad+(-1)^{\deg u}u\otimes\hat{d}({\bf v})\otimes w&\text{if ${\bf x}={\bf y}=1$},\\
0&\text{otherwise},\end{cases}\hskip-28.45274pt

(10.7)

where
*=\deg u+\deg^{\prime}{\bf v}_{a;1}

and
\Delta{\bf v}=\sum_{a}\text{\bf v}_{a;1}\otimes\text{\bf v}_{a;2}
.
It is straightforward to check (
5.12
) with sign modified
(see Remark
10.7
).
We thus defined
\mathfrak{ten}_{\rm ob}
.

Remark 10.7
.

We remark that in the second, fourth and fifth lines of the right-hand side
we used
\deg u
and not
\deg^{\prime}u
.

Remark 10.8
.

Note that in the case of
\mathfrak{D}^{13}=\mathfrak{ten}\bigl(\mathfrak{D}^{12},\mathfrak{D}^{23}\bigr)
, the ‘left multiplication’ and
the ‘right multiplication’ exactly commute.
This is the reason why we take
0
in the fourth case of (
10.7
).
In fact,
\mathfrak{n}_{1,1}
in the bi-module structure is a chain homotopy between

\mathfrak{n}_{0,1}(\mathfrak{n}_{1,0}(x,z),y)
and
{(-1)^{\deg^{\prime}x}\mathfrak{n}_{1,0}(x,\mathfrak{n}_{0,1}(z,y))}
.

We next define the morphism part of the bi-functor
\mathfrak{ten}
.
Let

\mathfrak{D}^{(j),12}=\bigl(\bigl\{D^{(j),12}_{c_{1},c_{2}}\bigr\},\bigl\{\mathfrak{n}^{12}_{c_{1}^{\prime},c_{1},c_{2},c_{2}^{\prime}}\bigr\}\bigr)

be an object of

\mathcal{BIMOD}(\mathscr{C}_{1},\mathscr{C}_{2})
for
j=1,2

and

\mathfrak{D}^{(j),23}=\bigl(\bigl\{D^{(j),23}_{c_{2},c_{3}}\bigr\},\bigl\{\mathfrak{n}^{23}_{c_{2}^{\prime},c_{2},c_{3},c_{3}^{\prime}}\bigr\}\bigr)

an object of

\mathcal{BIMOD}(\mathscr{C}_{1},\mathscr{C}_{2})
for
j=1,2
.
A pre-bi-module homomorphism

\mathfrak{f}^{12}\colon\mathfrak{D}^{(1),12}\!\to\mathfrak{D}^{(2),12}

(resp.
\mathfrak{f}^{23}\colon\mathfrak{D}^{(1),23}\to\mathfrak{D}^{(2),23}
) consists of

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\mathfrak{f}^{12}_{k_{1},k_{2}}\colon\ B_{k_{1}}\mathscr{C}_{1}[1](c_{1},c^{\prime}_{1})\otimes D^{(1),12}_{c^{\prime}_{1},c^{\prime}_{2}}\otimes B_{k_{2}}\mathscr{C}_{2}[1](c^{\prime}_{2},c_{2})\to D^{(2),12}_{c_{1},c_{2}},\\
&\text{(resp.}\ \mathfrak{f}^{23}_{k_{2},k_{3}}\colon\ B_{k_{2}}\mathscr{C}_{2}[1](c_{2},c^{\prime}_{2})\otimes D^{(1),23}_{c^{\prime}_{2},c^{\prime}_{3}}\otimes B_{k_{3}}\mathscr{C}_{3}[1](c^{\prime}_{3},c_{3})\to D^{(2),23}_{c_{2},c_{3}}).\end{split}

See Definition
5.11
.
We define its tensor product

\mathfrak{f}^{12}\otimes\mathfrak{f}^{23}=\mathfrak{f}^{13}
as follows.
We define
D^{(j),13}_{c_{1},c_{3}}
in the same way as (
10.6
).

\mathfrak{f}^{13}
consists of the maps

\mathfrak{f}^{13}_{k_{1},k_{3}}\colon\ B_{k_{1}}\mathscr{C}_{1}[1](c_{1},c^{\prime}_{1})\otimes D^{(1),13}_{c^{\prime}_{1},c^{\prime}_{3}}\otimes B_{k_{3}}\mathscr{C}_{3}[1](c^{\prime}_{3},c_{3})\to D^{(2),13}_{c_{2},c_{3}},

which we define by the next formula.
Let
{\bf x}\in B\mathscr{C}_{1}[1](c^{\prime}_{1},c_{1})
,

{\bf y}\in B\mathscr{C}_{3}[1](c_{3},c^{\prime}_{3})

and

z=u\otimes{\bf v}\otimes w\in D^{(1),12}_{c_{1},c_{2}}\,\widehat{\otimes}\,B\mathscr{C}_{2}[1](c_{2},c^{\prime}_{2})\,\widehat{\otimes}\,D^{23}_{c^{\prime}_{2},c_{3}}\subset D^{(1),13}_{c_{1},c_{3}}.

We put

\displaystyle\mathfrak{f}^{13}_{k_{1},k_{3}}({\bf x},z,{\bf y})

\displaystyle\qquad=\sum_{a}(-1)^{\deg\mathfrak{f}^{23}_{*,k_{3}}(\deg^{\prime}{\bf x}+\deg u+\deg^{\prime}{\bf v}_{a;1}+\deg^{\prime}{\bf v}_{a;2})}\mathfrak{f}^{12}_{k_{1},*}({\bf x},u,{\bf v}_{a;1})\otimes{\bf v}_{a;2}\otimes\mathfrak{f}^{23}_{*,k_{3}}({\bf v}_{a;3},w,{\bf y}).

Here
(1\otimes\Delta)\circ\Delta{\bf v}=\sum_{a}\text{\bf v}_{a;1}\otimes\text{\bf v}_{a;2}\otimes\text{\bf v}_{a;3}
.
We can easily show that
\mathfrak{f}^{13}
gives a chain map

\displaystyle\mathcal{BIMOD}(\mathscr{C}_{1},\mathscr{C}_{2})\bigl(\mathfrak{D}^{(1),12},\mathfrak{D}^{(2),12}\bigr)\otimes\mathcal{BIMOD}(\mathscr{C}_{2},\mathscr{C}_{3})\bigl(\mathfrak{D}^{(1),23},\mathfrak{D}^{(2),23}\bigr)

\displaystyle\qquad\to\mathcal{BIMOD}(\mathscr{C}_{1},\mathscr{C}_{3})\bigl(\mathfrak{D}^{(1),13},\mathfrak{D}^{(2),13}\bigr)

Moreover, this map
\bigl(\mathfrak{f}^{12},\mathfrak{f}^{23}\bigr)\mapsto\mathfrak{f}^{12}\otimes\mathfrak{f}^{23}=\mathfrak{f}^{13}

is compatible with composition. Namely,

\displaystyle\bigl(\mathfrak{f}^{(1),12}\circ\mathfrak{f}^{(2),12}\bigr)\otimes_{s}\bigl(\mathfrak{f}^{(1),23}\circ\mathfrak{f}^{(2),23}\bigr)

\displaystyle\qquad=(-1)^{\deg\mathfrak{f}^{(1),23}\deg\mathfrak{f}^{(2),12}}\bigl(\mathfrak{f}^{(1),12}\otimes_{s}\mathfrak{f}^{(1),23}\bigr)\circ\bigl(\mathfrak{f}^{(2),12}\otimes_{s}\mathfrak{f}^{(2),23}\bigr).

See (
2.11
) for
\otimes_{s}
.

Therefore, by putting other operations to be zero we obtain a required bi-functor.
The proof of Lemma–Definition
10.6
is complete.
∎

The derived tensor product functor induces

\mathcal{BIMOD}(\mathscr{C}_{1},\mathscr{C}_{2})^{\rm op}\times\mathcal{BIMOD}(\mathscr{C}_{2},\mathscr{C}_{3})^{\rm op}\to\mathcal{BIMOD}(\mathscr{C}_{1},\mathscr{C}_{3})^{\rm op},

which we denote also by
\mathfrak{ten}
.

Remark 10.9
.

The proof shows that
\mathfrak{ten}
is actually a bi-DG-functor between DG-categories.

The proof of the next proposition is the most nontrivial
part of the proof of Theorem
10.1
.

Proposition 10.10
.

Assume that
\mathscr{C}_{1}
,
\mathscr{C}_{2}
,
\mathscr{C}_{3}

are unital, strict and gapped.
Let
\mathscr{F}_{12}\colon\mathscr{C}_{1}\to\mathscr{C}_{2}

and
\mathscr{F}_{23}\colon\mathscr{C}_{2}\to\mathscr{C}_{3}

be filtered
A_{\infty}
functors.
Then the object

\mathfrak{ten}_{\rm ob}(\mathfrak{RYon}_{\rm ob}(\mathfrak{F}_{12}),\mathfrak{RYon}_{\rm ob}(\mathfrak{F}_{23}))

of

\mathcal{BIMOD}(\mathscr{C}_{1},\mathscr{C}_{3})^{\rm op}
is homotopy equivalent to

\mathfrak{RYon}_{\rm ob}(\mathfrak{F}_{23}\circ\mathfrak{F}_{12})
.

The proof is given in Section
10.5
.

Remark 10.11
.

Suppose that
\mathscr{C}_{1}
,
\mathscr{C}_{2}
and
\mathscr{C}_{3}

are associative rings with unity. They can be regarded as unital
A_{\infty}
categories.
Let
\mathscr{F}_{12}\colon\mathscr{C}_{1}\to\mathscr{C}_{2}

and
\mathscr{F}_{23}\colon\mathscr{C}_{2}\to\mathscr{C}_{3}
be unital ring homomorphisms
which are special cases of
A_{\infty}
functors.

The bi-module associated to
\mathscr{F}_{12}
is
\mathscr{C}_{2}

which is regarded as a right
\mathscr{C}_{2}
module by right multiplication
and a left
\mathscr{C}_{1}
module by
x\cdot y=\mathscr{F}_{12}(x)y
.
We write this bi-module as
{}_{\mathscr{C}_{1}}(\mathscr{C}_{2})_{\mathscr{C}_{2}}
.
In the same way
\mathscr{F}_{23}
corresponds to

{}_{\mathscr{C}_{2}}(\mathscr{C}_{3})_{\mathscr{C}_{3}}
.
Their tensor product is

{}_{\mathscr{C}_{1}}(\mathscr{C}_{2})_{\mathscr{C}_{2}}\otimes_{\mathscr{C}_{2}}{}_{\mathscr{C}_{2}}(\mathscr{C}_{3})_{\mathscr{C}_{3}}={}_{\mathscr{C}_{1}}(\mathscr{C}_{3})_{\mathscr{C}_{3}}
.
Here the left
\mathscr{C}_{1}
module structure is induced by
\mathscr{F}_{23}\circ\mathscr{F}_{12}
.
This is Proposition
10.10
in this case.

Now we are in the position to complete the proof of
Theorem
10.1
.
We consider the bi-functor

\mathfrak{ten}\circ(\mathfrak{RYon}\times\mathfrak{RYon})\colon\ \mathcal{FUNC}(\mathscr{C}_{1},\mathscr{C}_{2})\times\mathcal{FUNC}(\mathscr{C}_{2},\mathscr{C}_{3})\to\mathcal{BIMOD}(\mathscr{C}_{1},\mathscr{C}_{3})^{\rm op}.

We consider the full subcategory

\mathfrak{Rep}(\mathscr{C}_{1},\mathscr{C}_{3})

of

\mathcal{BIMOD}(\mathscr{C}_{1},\mathscr{C}_{3})^{\rm op}

whose object is homotopy equivalent to an image
of

\mathfrak{RYon}\colon\mathcal{FUNC}(\mathscr{C}_{1},\mathscr{C}_{3})\to\mathcal{BIMOD}(\mathscr{C}_{1},\mathscr{C}_{3})^{\rm op}
.
Proposition
10.10
implies that the image of

\mathfrak{ten}\circ(\mathfrak{RYon}\times\mathfrak{RYon})

is contained in this full subcategory.

Moreover, by Lemma–Definition
10.3

and Theorem
2.28
,
there exists a filtered
A_{\infty}
functor
{\mathfrak{Rep}(\mathscr{C}_{1},\mathscr{C}_{3})\to\mathcal{FUNC}(\mathscr{C}_{1},\mathscr{C}_{3})}

which is a homotopy inverse to
\mathfrak{RYon}
.
Therefore, there exists a filtered
A_{\infty}

functor

\mathfrak{Comp}\colon\mathcal{FUNC}(\mathscr{C}_{1},\mathscr{C}_{2})\times\mathcal{FUNC}(\mathscr{C}_{2},\mathscr{C}_{3})\to\mathcal{FUNC}(\mathscr{C}_{1},\mathscr{C}_{3})

such that the next diagram commutes up to homotopy equivalence:

\begin{CD}\mathcal{FUNC}(\mathscr{C}_{1},\mathscr{C}_{2})\times\mathcal{FUNC}(\mathscr{C}_{2},\mathscr{C}_{3})@ >>>\mathcal{FUNC}(\mathscr{C}_{1},\mathscr{C}_{3})\\
@ VVV@ VVV\\
\mathcal{BIMOD}(\mathscr{C}_{1},\mathscr{C}_{2})^{\rm op}\times\mathcal{BIMOD}(\mathscr{C}_{2},\mathscr{C}_{3})^{\rm op}@ >>>\mathcal{BIMOD}(\mathscr{C}_{1},\mathscr{C}_{3})^{\rm op}.\end{CD}

(10.8)

This is the required functor.
∎

Remark 10.12
.

The construction of the composition functor we gave in this subsection is rather indirect.
In other words, we did not provide an explicit formula how the pre-natural transformations
are sent by this functor. This is because an explicit homotopy inverse to the Yoneda
functor is not given. We can certainly find some formula by following the proof.
In fact, the Yoneda functor is explicitly
given in
[
fu4
]
and the proof of Theorem
2.28
in
[
fu4
]

is by induction each of whose steps is in principle can be made explicit.
However, the explicit formula which we may obtain in that way seems to be very complicated
and is not practical to use it.

10.2
Proof of Theorem
9.1

In this section, we prove Theorem
9.1
.
Before starting the proof, we twist the (category version of the) map
\mathscr{YT}
in
Proposition
9.12
by bounding cochains.
We denote by
\mathcal{L}_{i}
,
\mathcal{L}_{ii^{\prime}}

or
\mathcal{L}^{(j)}_{i}
,
\mathcal{L}^{(j)}_{ii^{\prime}}
objects of

\mathfrak{Fukst}(X_{i})
,
\mathfrak{Fukst}(-X_{i}\times X_{i^{\prime}})
.
We recall

B_{k}CF[1](\mathcal{L}_{i},\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{i})=\bigoplus_{\mathcal{L}_{i}=\mathcal{L}^{(0)}_{i},\dots,\mathcal{L}^{(k)}_{i}=\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{i}}\bigotimes_{j=1}^{k}CF\bigl(\mathcal{L}^{(j-1)}_{i},\mathcal{L}^{(j)}_{i}\bigr)[1]

and
BCF[1](\mathcal{L}_{i},\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{i})

is their completed direct sum over
k
.
We define the modules

B_{k}CF[1](\mathcal{L}_{ii^{\prime}},\allowbreak\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{ii^{\prime}})
,

BCF[1](\mathcal{L}_{ii^{\prime}},\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{ii^{\prime}})
in the same way.

We define a map

\mathfrak{t}_{\vec{b}}\colon\bigotimes_{j=1}^{k}CF\bigl(\mathcal{L}^{(j-1)}_{i},\mathcal{L}^{(j)}_{i}\bigr)[1]\to BCF[1](\mathcal{L}_{i},\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{i})

by

\mathfrak{t}_{\vec{b}}(x_{1},\dots,x_{k}):=e^{b_{0}}x_{1}e^{b_{1}}x_{2}\cdots x_{k-1}e^{b_{k-1}}x_{k}e^{b_{k}}

(see (
5.9
)).
It induces

\mathfrak{t}_{\vec{b}}\colon BCF[1](\mathcal{L}_{i},\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{i})\to BCF[1](\mathcal{L}_{i},\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{i})
.
We define

\mathfrak{t}_{\vec{b}}\colon BCF[1](\mathcal{L}_{ii^{\prime}},\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{ii^{\prime}})\to BCF[1](\mathcal{L}_{ii^{\prime}},\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{ii^{\prime}})
in the same way.

We now define the map

\displaystyle\mathscr{YT}^{\vec{b}}\colon\ BCF[1](\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{1})\otimes BCF[1](\mathcal{L}_{12},\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{12})\otimes BCF[1](\mathcal{L}_{23},\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{23})

\displaystyle\hphantom{\mathscr{YT}^{\vec{b}} \colon}\,\otimes CF(\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{1},\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{12};\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{2})\otimes BCF[1](\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{2},\mathcal{L}_{2})\otimes CF(\mathcal{L}_{2},\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{23};\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{3})\otimes BCF[1](\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{3},\mathcal{L}_{3})

\displaystyle\hphantom{\mathscr{YT}^{\vec{b}} \colon}\,\otimes BCF[1](\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{13},\mathcal{L}_{13})\otimes CF(\mathcal{L}_{13};\mathcal{L}_{12},\mathcal{L}_{23})\to CF(\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{13};\mathcal{L}_{3})

(10.9)

by composing
\mathfrak{t}_{\vec{b}}
with
\mathscr{YT}
.
(We do not apply
\mathfrak{t}_{\vec{b}}
to the factors

CF(\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{1},\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{12};\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{2})
,
CF(\mathcal{L}_{2},\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{23};\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{3})
,

CF(\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{13};\mathcal{L}_{3})
.)

Lemma 10.13
.

(
9.25
) holds when we replace
\mathscr{YT}
,

\hat{d}
,
\mathfrak{n}
by

\mathscr{YT}^{\vec{b}}
,
\hat{d}^{b}
,
\mathfrak{n}^{b}
,
respectively.
Here
\hat{d}^{b}
,
\mathfrak{n}^{b}
are defined by

\mathfrak{t}_{\vec{b}}\circ\hat{d}^{b}=\hat{d}\circ\mathfrak{t}_{\vec{b}}
,

\mathfrak{t}_{\vec{b}}\circ\mathfrak{n}^{b}=\mathfrak{n}\circ\mathfrak{t}_{\vec{b}}
.

This is immediate from Proposition
9.12
.

Proof of Theorem
9.1
.

Let
\mathcal{L}_{12}
,
\mathcal{L}_{23}
be as in Theorem
9.1

and
\mathcal{L}_{13}=\mathcal{L}_{23}\circ\mathcal{L}_{12}
.
We apply the relative Yoneda functor
\mathfrak{RYon}_{\rm ob}
to

\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{L}_{13}}
. By definition we obtain

\mathscr{CF}(\mathbb{L}_{1},\mathbb{L}_{13};\mathbb{L}_{3}).

We fixed
{\mathcal{L}_{13}\in\mathbb{L}_{13}}

so
we regard
\mathscr{CF}(\mathbb{L}_{1},\mathbb{L}_{13};\mathbb{L}_{3})
as a left-
\mathfrak{Fukst}(X_{1})

and right-
\mathfrak{Fukst}(X_{3})
bi-module.
It assigns

\mathcal{W}^{(1)}(\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{3})=CF(\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{13};\mathcal{L}_{3})

to
\mathcal{L}_{i}\in\mathfrak{Ob}(\mathfrak{Fukst}(X_{i}))
for
i=1,3
.

We apply the relative Yoneda functor
\mathfrak{RYon}_{\rm ob}
to

\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{L}_{12}}
and
\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{L}_{23}}
.
We then obtain tri-modules
{\mathscr{CF}(\mathbb{L}_{1},\mathbb{L}_{12};\mathbb{L}_{2})}

and

\mathscr{CF}(\mathbb{L}_{2},\mathbb{L}_{23};\mathbb{L}_{3})
,
respectively.
We fix
\mathcal{L}_{12}
and
\mathcal{L}_{23}

and regard them as left-
\mathfrak{Fukst}(X_{1})

and right-
\mathfrak{Fukst}(X_{2})

and
left-
\mathfrak{Fukst}(X_{2})

and right-
\mathfrak{Fukst}(X_{3})

modules respectively.
We consider
\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{L}_{23}}\circ\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{L}_{12}}

and apply the relative Yoneda functor
\mathfrak{RYon}_{\rm ob}
to it.
Then, by Proposition
10.10
,
we obtain

\mathfrak{ten}(\mathscr{CF}(\mathbb{L}_{1},\mathbb{L}_{12};\mathbb{L}_{2}),\mathscr{CF}(\mathbb{L}_{2},\mathbb{L}_{23};\mathbb{L}_{3}))
.
We regard it as a left-
\mathfrak{Fukst}(X_{1})

and right-
\mathfrak{Fukst}(X_{3})
bi-module.
To
\mathcal{L}_{i}\in\mathfrak{Ob}(\mathfrak{Fukst}(X_{i}))
for
i=1,3
,
it assigns

\mathcal{W}^{(2)}(\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{3})=\bigoplus_{\mathcal{L}_{2},\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{2}}CF(\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{12};\mathcal{L}_{2})\otimes BCF[1](\mathcal{L}_{2},\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{2})\otimes CF(\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{2},\mathcal{L}_{23};\mathcal{L}_{3}).

(10.10)

The pre-bi-module homomorphism we look for is a
system of maps

BCF[1](\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{1})\otimes\mathcal{W}^{(2)}(\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{1},\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{3})\otimes BCF[1](\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{3},\mathcal{L}_{3})\to\mathcal{W}^{(1)}(\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{3}).

Namely,

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}\colon\ BCF[1](\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{1})

\displaystyle\otimes CF(\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{12};\mathcal{L}_{2})\otimes BCF[1](\mathcal{L}_{2},\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{2})

\displaystyle\otimes CF(\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{2},\mathcal{L}_{23};\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{3})\otimes BCF[1](\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{3},\mathcal{L}_{3})\to CF(\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{13};\mathcal{L}_{3}).

We define

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}({\bf h}_{1},h_{\infty,+,12},{\bf h}_{2},h_{\infty,+,23},{\bf h}_{3})

\displaystyle\qquad=(-1)^{*}\mathscr{YT}^{\vec{b}}({\bf 1}_{123};\varnothing_{12},\varnothing_{23},\varnothing_{13};h_{\infty,+,12},h_{\infty,+,23};{\bf h}_{1},{\bf h}_{2},{\bf h}_{3}).

Here
\varnothing_{ii^{\prime}}=1\in B_{0}CF(\mathcal{L}_{ii^{\prime}})
and

{\bf 1}_{123}\in CF(\mathcal{L}_{12},\mathcal{L}_{23};\mathcal{L}_{13})
is the cyclic
element. The sign
(-1)^{*}
is determined by the Koszul rule. We
count the way exchanging the order of the variables using the shifted degree
\deg^{\prime}

for elements of
BCF[1](\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{i},\mathcal{L}_{i})

(or
BCF[1](\mathcal{L}_{i},\mathcal{L}_{i})
)
and
\deg
for elements of
CF(\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{12};\mathcal{L}_{2})
etc.
Then we put the sign according to whether the total count is even or odd.

Remark 10.14
.

The sign in (
10.13
) is also by Koszul rule. However, it is different
from the one we describe above. Namely,
\deg^{\prime}
is used
also for elements of tri-modules,
CF(\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{12};\mathcal{L}_{2})

etc. We change the sign of the maps in the same way as
(
5.8
) (see also (
10.2
))
to go from one to the other.

The condition that
\mathcal{T}
is a bi-module homomorphism
is a consequence of Lemma
10.13
and the fact that
{\bf 1}_{123}
becomes a cycle (after twisting
the boundary operator by the bounding cochains
b_{12}
,
b_{23}
,
b_{13}
).

We continue the proof of Theorem
9.1

and prove that
\mathcal{T}
is a homotopy equivalence.
In view of Lemma
7.9
, the next step is to prove that
the chain map

\mathcal{T}_{0,0;\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{3}}\colon\ \mathcal{W}^{(2)}(\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{3})\to\mathcal{W}^{(1)}(\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{3})

(10.11)

is a chain homotopy equivalence for arbitrary

\mathcal{L}_{1}
,
\mathcal{L}_{3}
.
By Proposition
10.10
,
the derived tensor product (
10.10
)
is chain homotopy equivalent to

CF(\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{L}_{23}}(\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{L}_{12}}(\mathcal{L}_{1})),\mathcal{L}_{3})\cong CF(\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{L}_{12}}(\mathcal{L}_{1}),\mathcal{L}_{23};\mathcal{L}_{3}).

(10.12)

In fact, the chain homotopy equivalence from (
10.12
) to
(
10.10
) is given by

x\mapsto{\bf 1}_{12}\otimes x,

(10.13)

for
x\in CF(\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{L}_{23}}(\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{L}_{12}}(\mathcal{L}_{1})),\mathcal{L}_{3})
.
Here
{\bf 1}_{12}\in CF(\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{12};\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{L}_{12}}(\mathcal{L}_{1}))

is the cyclic element which becomes the unity
in
CF(\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{L}_{12}}(\mathcal{L}_{1}))
by the isomorphism

CF(\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{12};\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{L}_{12}}(\mathcal{L}_{1}))\cong CF(\mathcal{\mathcal{}}W_{\mathcal{L}_{12}}(\mathcal{L}_{1})).

Note that if we regard
x
as an element of the right-hand side of (
10.12
),
then
{\bf 1}_{12}\otimes x
is an element of

CF(\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{12};\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{L}_{12}}(\mathcal{L}_{1}))\otimes CF(\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{L}_{12}}(\mathcal{L}_{1}),\mathcal{L}_{23};\mathcal{L}_{3}),

which is contained in (
10.10
) as the case

\mathcal{L}_{2}=\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{2}=\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{L}_{12}}(\mathcal{L}_{1})
.

The map (
10.13
) is identified with the map

\mathscr{I}_{12;0,0}
in (
10.33
), which we will use to
prove Proposition
10.10
.

Thus to prove that (
10.11
) is a chain homotopy equivalence, it suffices to show that the composition

CF(\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{L}_{12}}(\mathcal{L}_{1}),\mathcal{L}_{12};\mathcal{L}_{3})\to\mathcal{W}^{(2)}(\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{3})\to\mathcal{W}^{(1)}(\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{3})

(10.14)

is a chain homotopy equivalence. By definition,
(
10.14
) is the map

\displaystyle h_{\infty,+,23}\mapsto\mathcal{T}(\varnothing_{1},{\bf 1}_{12},\varnothing_{2},h_{\infty,+,23},\varnothing_{3})

\displaystyle\hphantom{h_{\infty,+,23} \mapsto}{}=\mathscr{YT}^{\vec{b}}({\bf 1}_{123};\varnothing_{12},\varnothing_{23},\varnothing_{13};{\bf 1}_{12},h_{\infty,+,23};\varnothing_{1},\varnothing_{2},\varnothing_{3}).

(10.15)

Here
\varnothing_{i}=1\in B_{0}CF(\mathcal{L}_{i})
, for
i=1,2,3
.

Lemma 10.15
.

(1)

CF(\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{L}_{12}}(\mathcal{L}_{1}),\mathcal{L}_{23};\mathcal{L}_{3})\cong\mathcal{W}^{(1)}(\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{3})\cong\Omega({L_{1}\times_{X_{1}}L_{12}\times_{X_{2}}L_{23}\times_{X_{3}}L_{3}},\Theta_{-})\,\widehat{\otimes}\,\Lambda_{0}
.

(2)

The map (
10.15
) is congruent to the identity map modulo
\Lambda_{+}

via the isomorphism of item
(1)
.

Proof.

(1) is immediate from the definition.
(2) then follows from the fact that energy
0
part of the map

\mathscr{FY}^{\vec{b}}
is defined by the moduli space of constant maps.
∎

To complete the proof of Theorem
9.1
,
we need to discuss the following point.
Note that while we proved Proposition

9.2
we showed that the two
bounding cochains, written as
b_{3}^{(1)}
and
b_{3}^{(2)}

there, are gauge equivalent. However, they are not necessary
equal.
In the above argument, we used
b_{3}^{(2)}
.
In fact,
CF(\mathcal{L}_{2},\mathcal{L}_{23};\mathcal{L}_{3})

using
b_{3}^{(2)}
gives

\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{L}_{23}}\colon\mathfrak{Fukst}(X_{2})\to\mathfrak{Fukst}(X_{3})
.

On the other hand,
CF(\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{13};\mathcal{L}_{3})

with
b_{3}^{(1)}
gives

\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{L}_{13}}\colon\mathfrak{Fukst}(X_{1})\to\mathfrak{Fukst}(X_{3})
.
Therefore, to complete the proof of
Theorem
9.1
, we need
to compare

CF(\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{13};\mathcal{L}_{3})

with two different choices of bounding cochains and show that they are
homotopy equivalent as left-
\mathfrak{Fukst}(X_{1})

and right-
\mathfrak{Fukst}(X_{3})
bi-modules.
We can prove it in the same way as the proof of
Proposition

9.2
as follows.

We consider

{\rm Poly}(CF(\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{13};\mathcal{L}_{3}))

which is a left-
\mathfrak{Fukst}(X_{1})
and right-
{\rm Poly}(\mathfrak{Fuk}(X_{3}))
bi-module.
(See
[
fooobook
, Section 5.2.3]
and the proof of
Proposition
6.16
.)
Here
{\rm Poly}(\mathfrak{Fuk}(X_{3}))
is an
A_{\infty}
category
obtained from
\mathfrak{Fuk}(X_{3})
replacing the morphism
modules
CF(\mathcal{L}_{3},\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{3})
by

{\rm Poly}(CF(\mathcal{L}_{3},\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{3}))
.

The
A_{\infty}
category
{\rm Poly}(\mathfrak{Fuk}(X_{3}))
is curved.
Note that each objects of
\mathfrak{Fukst}(X_{3})

which is in the image of the functors

\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{L}_{23}}\circ\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{L}_{12}}

(resp.
\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{L}_{13}}
)
comes with a choice of bounding cochains
b_{3}^{(2)}

(
resp.
b_{3}^{(1)}
)
.
We can lift those choices
to a bounding cochain
\hat{b}
such that

{\rm Eval}_{s=0}\bigl(\hat{b}\bigr)=b_{3}^{(1)}

and
{\rm Eval}_{s=1}\bigl(\hat{b}\bigr)=b_{3}^{(2)}
.
(See the proof of Lemma
9.14
.)
We use
\hat{b}
to eliminate curvature
and obtain an object of associated strict category
of
{\rm Poly}(\mathfrak{Fuk}(X_{3}))
, which we denote by

{\rm Poly}_{st}(\mathfrak{Fuk}(X_{3}))
.

By the proof of Proposition
9.2
,
there exists a commutative diagram of

A_{\infty}
functors

\begin{CD}\mathfrak{Fukst}(X_{1})@ >>>\mathcal{FUNC}(\mathfrak{Fukst}(X_{3})^{\rm op},\mathcal{CH})\\
@ V{=}VV@ VV{{\rm Eval}_{s=0}^{*}}V\\
\mathfrak{Fukst}(X_{1})@ >>>\mathcal{FUNC}({\rm Poly}_{st}(\mathfrak{Fuk}(X_{3}))^{\rm op},\mathcal{CH})\\
@ A{=}AA@ AA{{\rm Eval}_{s=1}^{*}}A\\
\mathfrak{Fukst}(X_{1})@ >>>\mathcal{FUNC}(\mathfrak{Fukst}(X_{3})^{\rm op},\mathcal{CH}).\end{CD}

(10.16)

Here the first horizontal arrow is
obtained by using
b_{3}^{(1)}
and the third horizontal arrow is
obtained by using
b_{3}^{(2)}
.
Since the right vertical arrows are
homotopy equivalences, we obtained
the required homotopy equivalence.

The proof of Theorem
9.1
is complete.
∎

10.3
The compatibility as bi-functors

We can strengthen Theorem
9.1
as follows.

Theorem 10.16
.

The next diagram commutes up to homotopy equivalence of
unital, strict and gapped filtered
A_{\infty}
bi-functors:

\begin{CD}\mathfrak{Fukst}(-X_{1}\times X_{2})\times\mathfrak{Fukst}(-X_{2}\times X_{3})@ >>>\mathfrak{Fukst}(-X_{1}\times X_{3})\\
@ VVV@ VVV\\
\displaystyle{\mathcal{FUNC}(\mathfrak{Fukst}(X_{1}),\mathfrak{Fukst}(X_{2}))\atop\times\mathcal{FUNC}(\mathfrak{Fukst}(X_{2}),\mathfrak{Fukst}(X_{3}))}@ >>>\mathcal{FUNC}(\mathfrak{Fukst}(X_{1}),\mathfrak{Fukst}(X_{3})).\end{CD}

Here the first horizontal arrow is (
8.3
)
and the second horizontal arrow is (
10.1
)
in the case of
{\mathscr{C}_{i}=\mathfrak{Fukst}(X_{i})}
.
The vertical arrows are correspondence bi-functors.

The proof will be given in Section
10.4
.

Remark 10.17
.

Theorem
10.16
enhances Theorem
9.1
,
and Theorem
9.1
enhances Proposition
9.2
.
Below we explain the difference between those three statements.
Theorem
10.16

is a coincidence between two
bi-functors

\mathfrak{Fukst}(-X_{1}\times X_{2})\times\mathfrak{Fukst}(-X_{2}\times X_{3})\to\mathcal{FUNC}(\mathfrak{Fukst}(X_{1}),\mathfrak{Fukst}(X_{3})).

(10.17)

We first fix an object
\mathcal{L}_{12}
(resp.
\mathcal{L}_{23}
)
of
\mathfrak{Fukst}(-X_{1}\times X_{2})
(resp.
\mathfrak{Fukst}(-X_{2}\times X_{3})
).
Then the two bi-functors (
10.17
) give two
objects of

\mathcal{FUNC}(\mathfrak{Fukst}(X_{1}),\mathfrak{Fukst}(X_{3}))
.
The coincidence of those two objects, which are the
functors:
\mathfrak{Fukst}(X_{1})\to\mathfrak{Fukst}(X_{3})
,
is Theorem
9.1
.
Note that a functor:
\mathfrak{Fukst}(X_{1})\to\mathfrak{Fukst}(X_{3})

gives a set theoretical map:

\mathfrak{OB}(\mathfrak{Fukst}(X_{1}))\to\mathfrak{OB}(\mathfrak{Fukst}(X_{3}))
.
The coincidence of two such set theoretical maps
is Proposition

9.2
.

Theorem
9.1
contains the coincidence of the morphism
parts of the functors:
\mathfrak{Fukst}(X_{1})\to\mathfrak{Fukst}(X_{3})
.
To prove Theorem
9.1
, we proved that (
10.11
)
is homotopy equivalence of left-
\mathfrak{Fukst}(X_{1})

and right-
\mathfrak{Fukst}(X_{3})
bi-modules.

Theorem
10.16
includes
statements on the coincidence of the way
the morphisms of
\mathfrak{Fukst}(-X_{1}\allowbreak\times X_{2})

and of
\mathfrak{Fukst}(-X_{2}\times X_{3})

are mapped by
(
10.17
).
In the homology level, it implies the following.
Suppose
\mathcal{L}_{12}
,
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{12}

(resp.
\mathcal{L}_{23}
,
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{23}
)
are objects of
\mathfrak{Fukst}(-X_{1}\times X_{2})

(resp.
\mathfrak{Fukst}(-X_{2}\times X_{3})
)
and
\mathcal{L}_{1}
,
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{1}

are objects of
\mathfrak{Fukst}(X_{1})
.

(
10.17
) defines a homomorphism

HF(\mathcal{L}_{12},\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{12})\otimes HF(\mathcal{L}_{23},\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{23})\to\operatorname{Hom}(HF(\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{1}),HF(\mathcal{L}_{3},\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{3})).

(10.18)

Here
\mathcal{L}_{3}
(resp.
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{3}
) is obtained by
transforming
\mathcal{L}_{1}
(resp.
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{1}
)
via the composition of
\mathcal{L}_{12}
and
\mathcal{L}_{23}

(resp.
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{12}
and
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{23}
).
Theorem
10.16
implies that
the homomorphisms (
10.18
) obtained
by the following two different ways coincide.

The first way to obtain (
10.18
) is the following.
Let
\mathcal{L}_{13}
(resp.
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{13}
)
be the composition of
\mathcal{L}_{12}
(resp.
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{12}
) and
\mathcal{L}_{23}
(resp.
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{23}
).
Then the composition bi-functor induces a homomorphism

HF(\mathcal{L}_{12},\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{12})\otimes HF(\mathcal{L}_{23},\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{23})\to HF(\mathcal{L}_{13},\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{13}).

(10.19)

On the other hand, by (
7.3
), we have

HF(\mathcal{L}_{13},\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{13})\to\operatorname{Hom}(HF(\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{1}),HF(\mathcal{L}_{3},\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{3})).

(10.20)

The composition of (
10.19
) and
(
10.20
) defines a homomorphism (
10.18
).

The second way to obtain (
10.18
) is the following.
We have the following homomorphisms from (
7.3
):

\displaystyle HF(\mathcal{L}_{12},\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{12})

\displaystyle\to\operatorname{Hom}(HF(\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{1}),HF(\mathcal{L}_{2},\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{2})),

\displaystyle HF(\mathcal{L}_{23},\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{23})

\displaystyle\to\operatorname{Hom}(HF(\mathcal{L}_{2},\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{2}),HF(\mathcal{L}_{3},\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{3})).

(10.21)

Here
\mathcal{L}_{12}
(resp.
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{12}
) transforms

\mathcal{L}_{1}
(resp.
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{1}
) to

\mathcal{L}_{2}
(resp.
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{2}
).
On the other hand, the composition of homomorphisms
define a homomorphism

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hom}(HF(\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{1}),HF(\mathcal{L}_{2},\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{2}))\otimes\operatorname{Hom}(HF(\mathcal{L}_{2},\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{2}),HF(\mathcal{L}_{3},\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{3}))

\displaystyle\qquad\to\operatorname{Hom}(HF(\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{1}),HF(\mathcal{L}_{3},\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{3})).

(10.22)

The composition of (
10.21
) and
(
10.22
) is the second way to obtain
(
10.18
).

To prove Theorem
10.16
, we need more homological algebra.
In Section
10.1
,
we used the derived tensor product to define the composition bi-functor
of functor categories.
In this subsection, we define the derived Hom functor.

Definition 10.18
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
and
\mathscr{C}_{(i)}
,
i=1,2

be strict, unital and gapped filtered
A_{\infty}
categories and

\mathfrak{D}_{1}
a left-
\mathscr{C}
,
\mathscr{C}_{(1)}

right-
\mathscr{C}_{(2)}
tri-module. For
c\in\mathfrak{OB}(\mathscr{C})
,
we define a left-
\mathscr{C}_{(1)}
right-
\mathscr{C}_{(2)}
bi-module

\mathfrak{D}|_{c}
as follows:

(1)

If
c_{i}\in\mathfrak{OB}(\mathscr{C}_{(i)})
, then

\mathfrak{D}|_{c}(c_{1},c_{2})=\mathfrak{D}(c,c_{1};c_{2})
.

(2)

For
{\bf x}\in B_{k_{1}}\mathscr{C}_{(1)}[1](c_{1};c^{\prime}_{1})
,

{\bf y}\in B_{k_{2}}\mathscr{C}_{(2)}[1](c^{\prime}_{2},c_{2})

and
v\in\mathfrak{D}|_{c}(c^{\prime}_{1},c^{\prime}_{2})=\mathfrak{D}(c,c^{\prime}_{1};c^{\prime}_{2})
,
we define
{\mathfrak{n}_{k_{1},k_{2}}({\bf x};v;{\bf y})\in\mathfrak{D}|_{c}(c_{1},c_{2})=\mathfrak{D}(c,c_{1};c_{2})}

by the tri-module structure on
\mathfrak{D}
.
This is the structure operation
\mathfrak{n}_{k_{1},k_{2}}
of
\mathfrak{D}|_{c}
.

Definition 10.19
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
and
\mathscr{C}_{(i)}
,
i=1,2,3,4
, be strict
filtered
A_{\infty}
categories
and
\mathfrak{D}_{1}
(resp.
\mathfrak{D}_{2}
) be a left-
\mathscr{C}
,
\mathscr{C}_{(1)}
right-
\mathscr{C}_{(2)}
(resp. left-
\mathscr{C}
,
\mathscr{C}_{(3)}
right-
\mathscr{C}_{(4)}
) filtered
A_{\infty}
tri-module.

Let
c_{i}\in\mathfrak{OB}(\mathscr{C}_{(i)})
.
We define
\mathfrak{Hom}_{\mathscr{C}}(\mathfrak{D}_{1},\mathfrak{D}_{2})(c_{2},c_{3};c_{1},c_{4})

as the set of objects

\mathfrak{f}=(\mathfrak{f}_{k_{2};c,c^{\prime}})_{c,c^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{OB}(\mathscr{C});k=0,1,2,\dots}

such that

\mathfrak{f}_{k;c,c^{\prime}}\colon B_{k}\mathscr{C}[1](c,c^{\prime})\otimes\mathfrak{D}_{1}|_{c^{\prime}}(c_{1},c_{2})\to\mathfrak{D}_{2}|_{c}(c_{3},c_{4})

is a filtered
\Lambda_{0}
module homomorphism.

Remark 10.20
.

We remark that
\mathfrak{Hom}_{\mathscr{C}}(\mathfrak{D}_{1},\mathfrak{D}_{2})(c_{2},c_{3};c_{1},c_{4})

is the direct
product

\prod_{c,c^{\prime}}\operatorname{Hom}(B_{k}\mathscr{C}[1](c,c^{\prime})\otimes\mathfrak{D}_{1}|_{c^{\prime}}(c_{1},c_{2}),\mathfrak{D}_{2}|_{c^{\prime}}(c_{3},c_{4})).

In the definition of derived tensor product, we used
direct
sum
, see (
10.6
).

Lemma–Definition 10.21
.

There exists a left-
\mathscr{C}_{(2)}
,
\mathscr{C}_{(3)}

right-
\mathscr{C}_{(1)}
,
\mathscr{C}_{(4)}
multi-module, denoted by

\mathfrak{Hom}_{\mathscr{C}}(\mathfrak{D}_{1},\mathfrak{D}_{2})
, so that

(c_{2},c_{3};c_{1},c_{4})\mapsto\mathfrak{Hom}_{\mathscr{C}}(\mathfrak{D}_{1},\mathfrak{D}_{2})(c_{2},c_{3};c_{1},c_{4})

in Definition
10.19
is its object part.

(
We define the boundary operator of the right-hand side during the proof.
)

We write it
\mathfrak{Hom}_{\mathscr{C}}(\mathfrak{D}_{1},\mathfrak{D}_{2})
and
call it the left
\mathscr{C}

hom-module
.

Proof.

Let
{\bf x}_{(1)}\in B_{k_{1}}\mathscr{C}_{(1)}[1](c_{1},c^{\prime}_{1})
,

{\bf y}_{(2)}\in B_{k_{2}}\mathscr{C}_{(2)}[1](c^{\prime}_{2},c_{2})
,

{\bf x}_{(3)}\in B_{k_{3}}\mathscr{C}_{(3)}[1](c_{3},c^{\prime}_{3})
,

{\bf y}_{(4)}\in B_{k_{4}}\mathscr{C}_{(4)}[1](c_{4},c^{\prime}_{4})
,
and
\mathfrak{f}\in\mathfrak{Hom}_{\mathscr{C}}(\mathfrak{D}_{1},\mathfrak{D}_{2})(c_{2},c_{3};c_{1},c_{4})
.

We define

\mathfrak{n}_{k_{1},k_{2},k_{3},k_{4}}({\bf y}_{(2)},{\bf x}_{(3)},\mathfrak{f},{\bf x}_{(1)},{\bf y}_{(4)})=\mathfrak{g}\in\mathfrak{Hom}_{\mathscr{C}}(\mathfrak{D}_{1},\mathfrak{D}_{2})(c^{\prime}_{2},c_{3};c^{\prime}_{1},c_{4})
,
as follows.
We put
\mathfrak{g}=0
if
k_{1}+k_{2}\neq 0
and
k_{3}+k_{4}\neq 0
.

If
k_{3}+k_{4}=0
and
k_{1}+k_{2}\neq 0
, we define

\displaystyle\mathfrak{n}_{k_{1},k_{2},0,0}({\bf y}_{(2)},\mathfrak{f},{\bf x}_{(1)})({\bf z},v)={}

\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}_{k;c_{2},c^{\prime}_{2}}({\bf z},v)

\displaystyle:={}

\displaystyle-\sum_{c}(-1)^{*}\mathfrak{f}({\bf z}_{c;1},\mathfrak{n}({\bf z}_{c;z,2},{\bf x}_{(1)},v,{\bf y}_{(2)})),

(10.23)

with

*=\deg^{\prime}{\bf z}_{c;1}+\deg\mathfrak{f}+\deg^{\prime}{\bf y}_{(2)}(\deg\mathfrak{f}+\deg v+\deg^{\prime}{\bf x}+\deg^{\prime}{\bf z})+\deg^{\prime}{\bf x}_{(1)}\deg^{\prime}{\bf z}.

Here
v\in D_{1}(c^{\prime},c^{\prime}_{2},c_{3};c^{\prime}_{1},c_{4})
,
{\bf z}\in B_{k}\mathscr{C}[1](c,c^{\prime})
,

\Delta{\bf z}=\sum_{c}{\bf z}_{c;1}\otimes{\bf z}_{c;2}

and
\mathfrak{n}
is the structure operation of
\mathfrak{D}_{1}
.

If
k_{3}+k_{4}\neq 0
and
k_{1}+k_{2}=0
, we define

\mathfrak{n}_{0,0,k_{3},k_{4}}({\bf x}_{(3)},\mathfrak{f},{\bf y}_{(4)})({\bf z},v)=\mathfrak{g}_{k;c_{2},c^{\prime}_{2}}({\bf z};v):=\sum_{c}(-1)^{*}\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}({\bf z}_{c;1},{\bf x}_{(3)},\mathfrak{f}({\bf z}_{c;2},v),{\bf y}_{(4)}),

(10.24)

with

*=\deg{\bf y}_{(4)}(\deg^{\prime}{\bf z}+\deg v)+\deg^{\prime}{\bf x}_{(3)}\deg^{\prime}{\bf z}_{c;1}+\deg\mathfrak{f}\deg^{\prime}{\bf z}_{c;1}
.
Here

v
,
{\bf z}
,

{\bf z}_{c;1},{\bf z}_{c;2}
are as above
and
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
is the structure operation of
\mathfrak{D}_{32}
.

If
k_{1}=k_{2}=k_{3}=k_{4}=0
, we put

\displaystyle\mathfrak{n}_{0,0}(1,\mathfrak{f},1)({\bf z};v)={}

\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}_{k_{2};c_{2},c^{\prime}_{2}}({\bf z};v)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\sum_{c}\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}({\bf z}_{c;1},\mathfrak{f}({\bf z}_{c;2},v))-(-1)^{\deg\mathfrak{f}+\deg^{\prime}{\bf z}_{c;1}}\sum_{c}\mathfrak{f}({\bf z}_{c;1},\mathfrak{n}({\bf z}_{c;2},v))

\displaystyle-(-1)^{\deg\mathfrak{f}}\mathfrak{f}\bigl(\hat{d}{\bf z},v\bigr).

(10.25)

Note that all the signs in (
10.23
), (
10.24
) and (
10.25
) are by Koszul rule.

We can check
A_{\infty}
relation as follows.
(Since the signs are by Koszul rule, the fact that the equality
holds
with signs
is in fact automatic.)
Let
\hat{d}
be a map from

\displaystyle\bigoplus_{c^{\prime}_{2},c^{\prime}_{1},c,c^{\prime}}B\mathscr{C}_{(2)}[1](c_{2},c^{\prime}_{2})

\displaystyle\qquad{}\otimes{\operatorname{Hom}}(B\mathscr{C}[1](c,c^{\prime})\otimes\mathfrak{D}_{1}(c^{\prime},c^{\prime}_{2},c_{3};c_{1},c_{4}),\mathfrak{D}_{2}(c,c_{2},c_{3};c^{\prime}_{1},c_{4}))\otimes B\mathscr{C}_{(1)}[1](c^{\prime}_{1},c_{1})

to itself which is the coderivation induced by the structure operations.
We will prove

\bigl(\mathfrak{n}\circ\hat{d}\bigr)({\bf y},\mathfrak{f},{\bf x})({\bf z};v)=0.

(10.26)

Suppose
k_{1}=k_{2}=k_{3}=k_{4}=0
for simplicity.
We have

\displaystyle\bigl(\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}\circ\hat{d}\bigr)(\mathfrak{f})({\bf z},v={}

\displaystyle\sum_{c}(-1)^{\deg^{\prime}{\bf z}_{c;1}(\deg\mathfrak{f}+1)}\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}({\bf z}_{c;1},{\mathfrak{n}(\mathfrak{f}})({\bf z}_{c;2},v))

\displaystyle+\sum_{c}(-1)^{\deg^{\prime}{\bf z}_{c;1}+\deg\mathfrak{f}}\mathfrak{n}(\mathfrak{f})({\bf z}_{c;1},\mathfrak{n}({\bf z}_{c;2},v))

\displaystyle+(-1)^{\deg\mathfrak{f}}(\mathfrak{n}(\mathfrak{f}))\bigl(\hat{d}{\bf z},v\bigr)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\sum_{c}(-1)^{\deg^{\prime}{\bf z}_{c;1}+\deg\mathfrak{f}(\deg^{\prime}{\bf z}_{c;1}+\deg^{\prime}{\bf z}_{c;2})}\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}({\bf z}_{c;1},\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}({\bf z}_{c;2},{\mathfrak{f}}({\bf z}_{c;3},v)))

\displaystyle+\sum_{c}(-1)^{\deg\mathfrak{f}+1+\deg^{\prime}{\bf z}_{c;2}+\deg^{\prime}{\bf z}_{c;1}(\deg\mathfrak{f}+1)}\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}({\bf z}_{c;1},\mathfrak{f}({\bf z}_{c;2},\mathfrak{n}({\bf z}_{c;3},v)))

\displaystyle+\sum_{c}(-1)^{\deg^{\prime}{\bf z}_{c;1}(\deg\mathfrak{f}+1)+(\deg\mathfrak{f}+1)}\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}\bigl({\bf z}_{c;1},\mathfrak{f}\bigl(\hat{d}{\bf z}_{c;2},v\bigr)\bigr)

\displaystyle+\sum_{c}(-1)^{\deg^{\prime}{\bf z}_{c;1}+\deg^{\prime}{\bf z}_{c;2}+\deg\mathfrak{f}+\deg^{\prime}{\bf z}_{c;1}\deg\mathfrak{f}}\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}({\bf z}_{c;1},\mathfrak{f}({\bf z}_{c;2},\mathfrak{n}({\bf z}_{c;3},v)))

\displaystyle+\sum_{c}(-1)^{\deg^{\prime}{\bf z}_{c;2}+1}\mathfrak{f}({\bf z}_{c;1},\mathfrak{n}({\bf z}_{c;2},\mathfrak{n}({\bf z}_{c;3},v)))

\displaystyle+\sum_{c}(-1)^{1+\deg^{\prime}{\bf z}_{c;1}}\mathfrak{f}\bigl(\hat{d}{\bf z}_{c;1},\mathfrak{n}({\bf z}_{c;2},v)\bigr)

\displaystyle+\sum_{c}(-1)^{\deg\mathfrak{f}+\deg\mathfrak{f}\deg^{\prime}{\bf z}_{c;1}+\deg^{\prime}{\bf z}_{c;1}}\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}\bigl({\bf z}_{c;1},\mathfrak{f}\bigl(\hat{d}{\bf z}_{c;2},v\bigr)\bigr)

\displaystyle+\sum_{c}(-1)^{\deg\mathfrak{f}+\deg\mathfrak{f}(\deg^{\prime}{\bf z}_{c;1}+1)}\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}\bigl(\hat{d}{\bf z}_{c;1},\mathfrak{f}({\bf z}_{c;2},v)\bigr)

\displaystyle+\sum_{c}(-1)^{1}\mathfrak{f}\bigl({\bf z}_{c;1},\mathfrak{n}\bigl(\hat{d}{\bf z}_{c;2},v\bigr)\bigr)

\displaystyle+\sum_{c}(-1)^{1+\deg^{\prime}{\bf z}_{c;1}+1}\mathfrak{f}(\hat{d}{\bf z}_{c;1},\mathfrak{n}({\bf z}_{c;2},v)).

The 1st and 8th terms of the right-hand side cancel by the
A_{\infty}
relation of
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
.
The 2nd and 4th terms cancel. The 3rd and 7th terms cancel.
The 5th and 9th terms cancel by the
A_{\infty}
relation of
\mathfrak{n}
.
The 6th and 10th terms cancel.
Thus we checked (
10.26
) in the case
k_{1}=k_{2}=k_{3}=k_{4}=0
.
The other cases are similar.
∎

Lemma–Definition 10.22
.

There exists a filtered
A_{\infty}
bi-functor

\mathcal{MUMOD}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{C}_{(1)};\mathscr{C}_{(2)})\times\mathcal{MUMOD}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{C}_{(3)};\mathscr{C}_{(4)})\to\mathcal{MUMOD}(\mathscr{C}_{(2)},\mathscr{C}_{(3)};\mathscr{C}_{(1)},\mathscr{C}_{(4)}),

which is given by Lemma–Definition
10.21
for the object part.

We call this bi-functor the
derived hom-functor

and write its as
\mathfrak{Hom}
.

Proof.

Let
\mathscr{C}
and
\mathscr{C}_{(i)}
,
i=1,2,3,4
, be strict
filtered
A_{\infty}
categories
and
\mathfrak{D}_{1}
,
\mathfrak{D}^{\prime}_{1}
(resp.
\mathfrak{D}_{2}
,
\mathfrak{D}^{\prime}_{2}
) be left-
\mathscr{C}
,
\mathscr{C}_{(1)}
right-
\mathscr{C}_{(2)}
(resp. left-
\mathscr{C}
,
\mathscr{C}_{(3)}
right-
\mathscr{C}_{(4)}
) filtered
A_{\infty}
tri-module.

Suppose

\mathfrak{F}_{1}\colon\mathfrak{D}^{\prime}_{1}\to\mathfrak{D}_{1}
and

\mathfrak{F}_{2}\colon\mathfrak{D}_{2}\to\mathfrak{D}^{\prime}_{2}
are tri-module homomorphisms.
We will define

(\mathfrak{F}^{*}_{1},\mathfrak{F}_{2*})\colon\ \mathfrak{Hom}_{\mathscr{C}_{2}}(\mathfrak{D}_{1},\mathfrak{D}_{2})\to\mathfrak{Hom}_{\mathscr{C}_{2}}(\mathfrak{D}^{\prime}_{1},\mathfrak{D}^{\prime}_{2}).

Let
\widehat{\mathfrak{f}}=(\mathfrak{f}^{c_{1},c_{2},c_{3},c_{4}})_{c_{i}\in\mathfrak{Ob}(\mathscr{C}_{(i)})};\mathfrak{Hom}_{\mathscr{C}}(\mathfrak{D}_{1},\mathfrak{D}_{2})
.
Here
\mathfrak{f}^{c_{1},c_{2},c_{3},c_{4}}=\bigl(\mathfrak{f}^{c_{1},c_{2},c_{3},c_{4}}_{c,c^{\prime}}\bigr)_{c,c^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{Ob}(\mathscr{C})}
and

\mathfrak{f}^{c_{1},c_{2},c_{3},c_{4}}_{c,c^{\prime}}\colon\ B\mathscr{C}[1](c,c^{\prime})\otimes\mathfrak{D}_{1}|_{c^{\prime}}(c_{2},c_{3};c_{1},c_{4})\to\mathfrak{D}_{2}|_{c^{\prime}}(c_{2},c_{3};c_{1},c_{4}).

We define

\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}=(\mathfrak{F}^{*}_{1},\mathfrak{F}_{2*})\bigl(\widehat{\mathfrak{f}}\bigr)
as follows.

\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}=(\mathfrak{g}^{c_{1},c_{2},c_{3},c_{4}})_{c_{i}\in\mathfrak{Ob}(\mathscr{C}_{(i)})}
,

\smash{\mathfrak{g}^{c_{1},c_{2},c_{3},c_{4}}=\bigl(\mathfrak{g}^{c_{1},c_{2},c_{3},c_{4}}_{c,c^{\prime}}};c,c^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{Ob}(\mathscr{C})\bigr)
,
and
\mathfrak{g}^{c_{1},c_{2},c_{3},c_{4}}_{c,c^{\prime}}\colon B\mathscr{C}(c,c^{\prime})\otimes\mathfrak{D}^{\prime}_{1}|_{c^{\prime}}(c_{1},c_{2};c_{3},c_{4})\to\mathfrak{D}^{\prime}_{2}|_{c}(c_{1},c_{2};c_{3},c_{4})
is

\mathfrak{g}^{c_{1},c_{2},c_{3},c_{4}}_{c,c^{\prime}}({\bf z},v):=\sum_{c}(-1)^{\deg\mathfrak{f}\deg^{\prime}{\bf z}_{c:1}}\mathfrak{F}_{2}({\bf z}_{c;1},\mathfrak{f}({\bf z}_{2;1},\mathfrak{F}_{1}({\bf z}_{c;3},v))).

Here
v\in\mathfrak{D}^{\prime}_{1}|_{c}(c_{1},c_{2};c_{3},c_{4})
,
{\bf z}\in B\mathscr{C}(c,c^{\prime})

and
((\Delta\otimes 1)\circ\Delta)({\bf y})=\sum_{c}{\bf z}_{c;1}\otimes{\bf z}_{c;2}\otimes{\bf z}_{c;3}
.

It is straightforward to check that
(\mathfrak{F}^{*}_{1},\mathfrak{F}_{2*})
is a chain map
and multi-module homomorphism.
Moreover, if
\mathfrak{F}_{1}\circ\mathfrak{G}_{1}=\mathfrak{H}_{1}
,

\mathfrak{G}_{2}\circ\mathfrak{F}_{2}=\mathfrak{H}_{2}
,
then

(\mathfrak{H}^{*}_{1},\mathfrak{H}_{2*})=(\mathfrak{G}^{*}_{1},\mathfrak{G}_{2*})\circ(\mathfrak{F}^{*}_{1},\mathfrak{F}_{2*})
.
Thus we obtain a required bi-functor.
(It is actually a DG-functor.)
∎

The next proposition is a Hom version of Proposition
10.10
.

Proposition 10.23
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
,
\mathscr{C}_{(i)}
,
i=1,2,3
, be strict unital and gapped
filtered
A_{\infty}
categories, and

\mathcal{F}\colon\mathscr{C}_{(1)}\to\mathscr{C}
and

\mathcal{G}\colon\mathscr{C}\times\mathscr{C}_{(2)}\to\mathscr{C}_{(3)}

strict, unital and gapped
filtered
A_{\infty}

(
bi-
)
functors.
We consider

\mathfrak{Yon}\circ\mathcal{F}\colon\mathscr{C}_{(1)}\to\mathcal{FUNC}(\mathscr{C}^{\rm op},\mathcal{CH})

and regard it as a bi-functor

\mathscr{C}^{\rm op}\times\mathscr{C}_{(1)}\to\mathcal{CH}
.
It can be regarded as a left-
\mathscr{C}
, right-
\mathscr{C}_{(1)}
,
bi-module, which we denote by
\mathfrak{D}_{(1)}
.
We apply
(
bi-module analogue of
)
the
relative Yoneda functor to
\mathcal{G}

to obtain
\mathfrak{RYon}_{\rm ob}(\mathcal{G})
, which becomes a left-
\mathscr{C}
,
\mathscr{C}_{(2)}
right
\mathscr{C}_{(3)}
tri-module,
which we denote by
\mathfrak{D}_{(2)}
.

We next consider the composition
\mathcal{G}\circ\mathcal{F}\colon\mathscr{C}_{(1)}\times\mathscr{C}_{(2)}\to\mathscr{C}_{(3)}
and
apply
(
the bi-module analogue of
)
the

We obtain a left-
\mathscr{C}_{(1)}
,
\mathscr{C}_{(2)}

.
Now we claim that

is homotopy equivalent
to

as
a left-
{\mathscr{C}}_{(1)}
,
{\mathscr{C}}_{(2)}

{\mathscr{C}}_{(3)}

Here the left
\mathscr{C}_{(1)}

\mathfrak{D}_{(1)}
.

(
We do not use left
\mathscr{C}

module structure on
\mathfrak{D}_{(2)}
to define this

\mathscr{C}_{(1)}
module structure.
)

We remark that by definition

\mathfrak{D}_{(3)}
is induced from
\mathfrak{D}_{(2)}

by
\mathcal{F}
.
The proof will be given in Section

.

Remark 10.24
.

We consider the case when
\mathscr{C}
and
\mathscr{C}_{(1)}
are unital associative algebras,

\mathscr{C}_{(2)}
is trivial,
and
\mathcal{F}
is a unital ring homomorphism.
We use the notation of Remark
10.11
.
Then
\mathfrak{D}_{(1)}
is the bi-module
{}_{\mathscr{C}}\mathscr{C}_{\mathscr{C}_{(1)}}

and
\mathfrak{D}_{(2)}
is given by a left
\mathscr{C}
right
{\mathscr{C}_{(3)}}
bimodule

{}_{\mathscr{C}}D_{\mathscr{C}_{(3)}}
.

Therefore,
\mathfrak{Hom}_{\mathscr{C}}(\mathfrak{D}_{(1)},\mathfrak{D}_{(2)})

is
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathscr{C}}({}_{\mathscr{C}}\mathscr{C}_{\mathscr{C}_{(1)}},{}_{\mathscr{C}}D_{\mathscr{C}_{(3)}})
.
The map sending
\varphi
to
\varphi({\bf e})
gives an isomorphism
between
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathscr{C}}({}_{\mathscr{C}}\mathscr{C}_{\mathscr{C}_{(1)}},{}_{\mathscr{C}}D_{\mathscr{C}_{(3)}})
and
{}_{\mathscr{C}_{(1)}}D_{\mathscr{C}_{(3)}}

as left
\mathscr{C}_{(1)}
right
\mathscr{C}_{(3)}
modules.
Note that the left
\mathscr{C}_{(1)}
action on
{}_{\mathscr{C}_{(1)}}D_{\mathscr{C}_{(3)}}

is defined by
\mathcal{F}\colon\mathscr{C}_{(1)}\to\mathscr{C}

and the left action of
\mathscr{C}
.

The bi-module
{}_{\mathscr{C}_{(1)}}D_{\mathscr{C}_{(3)}}
corresponds to the
composition
\mathcal{G}\circ\mathcal{F}
.
We thus checked Proposition
10.23
in this case.

10.1
10.1

10.1

Actually we use the variant of Proposition

.

Note that the quatro-module (

) associates

\displaystyle D_{2}(\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{12},\mathcal{L}_{23};\mathcal{L}_{3})

\displaystyle\qquad=\prod_{\mathcal{L}_{13},\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{13}}\operatorname{Hom}(BCF[1](\mathcal{L}_{13},\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{13})\otimes CF(\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{13};\mathcal{L}_{12},\mathcal{L}_{23}),CF(\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{13};\mathcal{L}_{3}))

to

We thus described two compositions

\mathfrak{Fukst}(-X_{1}\times X_{2})\times\mathfrak{Fukst}(-X_{2}\times X_{3})\to\mathcal{BIMOD}(\mathfrak{Fukst}(X_{1}),\mathfrak{Fukst}(X_{3}))^{\rm op},

which are (
10.29
) and (
10.32
) together with
their quatro-module structures.
Theorem
10.16

claims that they are homotopy equivalent as quatro-modules.
To prove it, we will construct a quatro-module homomorphism
from (
10.29
) to (
10.32
).

By definition, such a quatro-module homomorphism is a map

\displaystyle\bigoplus_{\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{1},\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{3},\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{12},\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{23}}BCF[1](\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{1})\otimes BCF[1](\mathcal{L}_{12},\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{12})\otimes BCF[1](\mathcal{L}_{23},\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{23})

\displaystyle\qquad{}\otimes D_{1}(\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{1},\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{12},\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{23};\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{3})\otimes BCF[1](\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{3},\mathcal{L}_{3})\to D_{2}(\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{12},\mathcal{L}_{23};\mathcal{L}_{3}).

Therefore, it can be regarded as a homomorphism from

\displaystyle BCF[1](\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{1})\otimes BCF[1](\mathcal{L}_{12},\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{12})\otimes BCF[1](\mathcal{L}_{23},\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{23})

\displaystyle\qquad{}\otimes CF(\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{1},\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{12};\mathcal{L}_{2})\otimes BCF[1](\mathcal{L}_{2},\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{2})\otimes CF(\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{2},\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{23};\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{3})\otimes BCF[1](\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{3},\mathcal{L}_{3})

\displaystyle\qquad{}\otimes BCF[1](\mathcal{L}_{13},\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{13})\otimes CF(\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{13};\mathcal{L}_{12},\mathcal{L}_{23})

to

CF(\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{13};\mathcal{L}_{3})
.
The
Y
-diagram transformation
\mathscr{YT}^{\vec{b}}
in (
10.9
)
is such a homomorphism and therefore defines a
pre-quatro-module homomorphism.
The condition that it becomes a quatro-module homomorphism
is exactly the formula (
9.24
), which we proved
in Lemma
10.13
.

To prove that this quatro-module homomorphism is a homotopy equivalence,
it suffices to show that the chain maps, which are parts of this
quatro-module homomorphism,
are chain homotopy equivalences
(see Proposition
7.9
).
The chain map induced by
\mathscr{YT}^{\vec{b}}
is
nothing but the chain homotopy equivalence (
10.14
)
which we produced during the proof of Theorem
9.1

in Section
10.2
.

We can study the difference between two bounding cochains

b_{3}^{(1)}
and
b_{3}^{(2)}
in the same way
as the last step of the proof of Theorem
9.1

by enhancing diagram (
10.16
), so that it includes
left-
\mathfrak{Fukst}(-X_{1}\times X_{2})
,
\mathfrak{Fukst}(-X_{2}\times X_{3})

structure.

The proof of Theorem
10.16
is now complete.
∎

Remark 10.25
.

To prove the commutativity of the diagram in Theorem
10.16

for the object part, it suffices to show that

D_{1}=CF(\mathcal{L}_{1};\mathcal{L}_{12};\mathcal{L}_{2})\otimes BCF[1](\mathcal{L}_{2})\otimes CF(\mathcal{L}_{2};\mathcal{L}_{23};\mathcal{L}_{3})

is homotopy equivalent to

D^{\prime}_{2}:=CF(\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{13};\mathcal{L}_{3})

as left-
\mathfrak{Fukst}(X_{1})
right-
\mathfrak{Fukst}(X_{3})

bi-modules.

To prove the commutativity of the morphism part,
we need to include the compatibility of the
homotopy equivalence with the left
\mathfrak{Fukst}(-X_{1}\times X_{2})\times\mathfrak{Fukst}(-X_{2}\times X_{3})

bi-module structures, as we have done above.

10.5
Proof of Propositions
10.10
and
10.23

In this subsection, we prove Propositions
10.10
and
10.23
.
We need certain calculations of the sign for the proof.
Note that in this paper the sign is almost always by the Koszul rule and
by this reason the cancellation with the sign is mostly automatic.
A certain nontrivial sign issue appears in this subsection
by the following reason.
We need to regard a filtered
A_{\infty}
category
\mathscr{C}

itself as a left-
\mathscr{C}
right-
\mathscr{C}
bi-module.
In such a case an element
v
of
\mathscr{C}(c,c^{\prime})
as an
element of bi-module appears with sign
(-1)^{\deg v}
in the
A_{\infty}

formula. In the case
v
is regarded as an element of a morphism
complex of an
A_{\infty}
category,
it appears with sign
(-1)^{\deg v+1}
in the
A_{\infty}

formula.

By several maps, which we will define in this subsection,
an element of
\mathscr{C}(c,c^{\prime})
as an
element of a bi-module in the domain becomes
an element of the morphism
complex in the co-domain or vice versa.
This process shifts the degree.
It is not obvious to understand the way how this process affects
the sign, since the Koszul rule does not tell it to us.
By this reason, we need to add a certain correction term
to the usual Koszul sign.
The author is unable to provide the general principle
on the way how the correction terms are determined.
Instead, he puts the correction terms ‘by hand’
(see, for example, (
10.37
)) and check that
the sign works by a calculation.
10.2
10.2

10.2

Actually a similar
problem occurs during the proof of Yoneda’s lemma.

Fortunately, this happens only in the purely algebraic situation
so that we do
not
need to understand the geometric
origin of the correction terms.
In fact, Propositions
10.10
and
10.23
are
algebraic statements and hold independent of the
origin of
A_{\infty}
categories and functors in their statements.
For the construction of various operations
using moduli spaces, the fundamental formulas
among those operations are always with Koszul sign.
We will use this fact in Section
.

Proof of Proposition
10.10
.

Let
\mathscr{F}_{i(i+1)}\colon\mathscr{C}_{i}\to\mathscr{C}_{i+1}

be a filtered
A_{\infty}
functor for
i=1,2
.
Let
c_{1}\in\mathfrak{OB}(\mathscr{C}_{1})
,
c_{3}\in\mathfrak{OB}(\mathscr{C}_{3})
.
We put

\displaystyle D^{1}(c_{1},c_{3}):=\mathscr{C}_{3}((\mathscr{F}_{23})_{\rm ob}((\mathscr{F}_{12})_{\rm ob}(c_{1})),c_{3}),

\displaystyle D^{2}(c_{1},c_{3}):=\bigoplus_{c_{2},c^{\prime}_{2}}\mathscr{C}_{2}((\mathscr{F}_{12})_{\rm ob}(c_{1}),c_{2})\,\widehat{\otimes}\,B\mathscr{C}_{2}(c_{2},c^{\prime}_{2})\,\widehat{\otimes}\,\mathscr{C}_{3}((\mathscr{F}_{23})_{\rm ob}(c^{\prime}_{2}),c_{3}).

Note that
D^{1}
is the object part of the bi-module

\mathfrak{RYon}_{\rm ob}(\mathfrak{F}_{23}\circ\mathfrak{F}_{12})
and

D^{2}
is the object part of the bi-module

\mathfrak{ten}_{\rm ob}(\mathfrak{RYon}_{\rm ob}(\mathfrak{F}_{12}),\mathfrak{RYon}_{\rm ob}(\mathfrak{F}_{23}))
.

We define

\mathscr{I}_{12;0,0}\colon D^{1}(c_{1},c_{3})\to D^{2}(c_{1},c_{3})

by

\mathscr{I}_{12;0,0}(z)={\bf e}_{(\mathscr{F}_{12})_{\rm ob}(c_{1})}\otimes 1\otimes z.

(10.33)

Here the symbol
{\bf e}_{(\mathscr{F}_{12})_{\rm ob}(c_{1})}
is the unity of the object

(\mathscr{F}_{12})_{\rm ob}(c_{1})
and the symbol
1
is an element of
B_{0}\mathscr{C}_{2}((\mathscr{F}_{12})_{\rm ob}(c_{1}),(\mathscr{F}_{12})_{\rm ob}(c_{1}))
, which is isomorphic to
\Lambda_{0}
. Hereafter, we omit
1
from the notation.
It is obvious that
\mathscr{I}_{12;0,0}
is a chain map.

We also define

\mathscr{I}_{21}\colon D^{2}(c_{1},c_{3})\to D^{1}(c_{1},c_{3})
by

\mathscr{I}_{21}(x,{\bf y},z)=(-1)^{\deg x}\mathfrak{m}_{*}\bigl(\widehat{\mathfrak{F}}_{23}(x,{\bf y}),z\bigr)
.
Here
\mathfrak{m}
is the structure operation of

\mathscr{C}_{3}
.

Let
\mathfrak{n}
is the
(0,0)
part of the
left-
\mathscr{C}_{1}
right-
\mathscr{C}_{3}
bi-module structure
of
D^{2}(c_{1},c_{3})
.
Here we use the sign
convention so that degree of elements of bi-module is
not
shifted.
Namely,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{n}(x\otimes{\bf y}\otimes z)={}

\displaystyle\sum_{c}(-1)^{\deg^{\prime}{\bf y}_{1;c}}\mathfrak{m}(x,{\bf y}_{1;c})\otimes{\bf y}_{2;c}\otimes z

\displaystyle+\sum_{c}(-1)^{\deg x+\deg^{\prime}{\bf y}_{1;c}}x\otimes{\bf y}_{1;c}\otimes\mathfrak{m}\bigl(\widehat{\mathfrak{F}}_{23}({\bf y}_{2;c}),z\bigr)

\displaystyle+(-1)^{\deg x}x\otimes\hat{d}({\bf y})\otimes z.

(10.34)

This is a special case of (
10.7
).
We will check that
\mathscr{I}_{21;0,0}
is a chain map.
We calculate

\displaystyle(\mathscr{I}_{21}\circ\mathfrak{n})(x,{\bf y},z)

\displaystyle\qquad=\sum_{c}(-1)^{\deg^{\prime}{\bf y}_{c;1}}\mathscr{I}_{21}(\mathfrak{m}(x,{\bf y}_{c;1}),{\bf y}_{c;2},z)

\displaystyle\phantom{\qquad=}{}+(-1)^{\deg x}\mathscr{I}_{21}\bigl(x,\hat{d}{\bf y},z\bigr)+\sum_{c}(-1)^{\deg x+\deg^{\prime}{\bf y}_{c;1}}\mathscr{I}_{21}\bigl(x,{\bf y}_{c;1},\mathfrak{m}\bigl(\widehat{\mathfrak{F}}_{23}({\bf y}_{c;2}),z\bigr)\bigr)

\displaystyle\qquad=\sum_{c}(-1)^{\deg x+1}\mathfrak{m}\bigl(\widehat{\mathfrak{F}}_{23}\bigl(\mathfrak{m}(x,{\bf y}_{c;1}),{\bf y}_{c;2}\bigr),z\bigr)

\displaystyle\phantom{\qquad=}{}+\mathfrak{m}\bigl(\widehat{\mathfrak{F}}_{23}\bigl(x,\hat{d}{\bf y}\bigr),z\bigr)+(-1)^{\deg^{\prime}{\bf y}_{c;1}}\sum_{c}\mathfrak{m}\bigl(\widehat{\mathfrak{F}}_{23}(x,{\bf y}_{c;1}),\mathfrak{m}\bigl(\widehat{\mathfrak{F}}_{23}({\bf y}_{c;2}),z\bigr)\bigr).

By
A_{\infty}
relation, this coincides with

(\mathfrak{n}\circ\mathscr{I}_{21})(x,{\bf y},z)=(-1)^{\deg x}\mathfrak{m}\bigl(\mathfrak{m}\bigl(\widehat{\mathfrak{F}}_{23}(x,{\bf y}),z\bigr)\bigr)
.

Lemma 10.26
.

\mathscr{I}_{12;0,0}
becomes a
(0,0)
part of a
filtered bi-module homomorphism.

Proof.

We first define

\mathscr{I}_{12;k_{1},k_{3}}\colon\ B_{k_{1}}\mathscr{C}_{1}[1](c_{1},c^{\prime}_{1})\,\widehat{\otimes}\,D^{1}(c^{\prime}_{1},c^{\prime}_{3})\,\widehat{\otimes}\,B_{k_{3}}\mathscr{C}_{3}[1](c^{\prime}_{3},c^{\prime}_{3})\to D^{2}(c_{1},c_{3})

.

A_{\infty}

Let

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathscr{I}}_{12}\colon\ \bigoplus_{c^{\prime}_{1},c^{\prime}_{3}}B\mathscr{C}_{1}[1](c_{1},c^{\prime}_{1})\,\widehat{\otimes}\,D^{1}(c^{\prime}_{1},c_{3})\,\widehat{\otimes}\,B\mathscr{C}_{3}[1](c^{\prime}_{3},c_{3})

\displaystyle\hphantom{\widehat{\mathscr{I}}_{12} \colon}\,\to\bigoplus_{c^{\prime}_{1},c^{\prime}_{3}}B\mathscr{C}_{1}[1](c_{1},c^{\prime}_{1})\,\widehat{\otimes}\,D^{2}(c^{\prime}_{1},c_{3})\,\widehat{\otimes}\,B\mathscr{C}_{3}[1](c^{\prime}_{3},c_{3})

be the formal bi-comodule homomorphism
induced by
\mathscr{I}_{12;k_{1},0}
,

.
Let
\hat{d}
be the
boundary operator on

\bigoplus_{c^{\prime}_{1},c^{\prime}_{3}}B\mathscr{C}_{1}[1](c_{1},c^{\prime}_{1})\,\widehat{\otimes}\,D^{i}(c^{\prime}_{1},c^{\prime}_{3})\,\widehat{\otimes}\,B\mathscr{C}_{3}[1](c^{\prime}_{3},c_{3})

induced by the bi-module structure
and

\mathfrak{n}\colon\ B\mathscr{C}_{1}[1](c_{1},c^{\prime}_{1})\,\widehat{\otimes}\,D^{i}(c^{\prime}_{1},c^{\prime}_{3})\,\widehat{\otimes}\,B\mathscr{C}_{3}[1](c^{\prime}_{3},c_{3})\to D^{i}(c^{\prime}_{1},c_{3}),

which is the structure operation of the bi-module structure as in (
10.7
).
Let
{\bf x}\in B\mathscr{C}_{1}[1](c_{1},c^{\prime}_{1})
,

z\in\mathscr{D}^{1}(c^{\prime}_{1},c^{\prime}_{3})
,

{\bf w}\in B_{k_{3}}\mathscr{C}_{3}[1](c^{\prime}_{3},c_{3})
.
We calculate

\displaystyle\bigl(\mathfrak{n}\circ\widehat{\mathscr{I}}_{12}\bigr)({\bf x},z,{\bf w})

\displaystyle\qquad=\sum_{c}\mathfrak{n}\bigl(\widehat{\mathscr{F}}_{12}({\bf x}_{c;1})\otimes\bigl({\bf e}\otimes\widehat{\mathscr{F}}_{12}({\bf x}_{c;2})\otimes z\bigr)\otimes{\bf w}\bigr)

\displaystyle\qquad=\begin{cases}\sum_{c}(-1)^{\deg^{\prime}{\bf x}_{c;1}}{\bf e}\otimes\widehat{\mathscr{F}}_{12}({\bf x}_{c;1})\otimes\mathfrak{m}\bigl(\bigl(\widehat{\mathscr{F}}_{23}\circ\widehat{\mathscr{F}}_{12}\bigr)({\bf x}_{c;2}),z,{\bf w}\bigr)&\text{if $k_{3}\neq 0$},\\
\sum_{c}(-1)^{\deg^{\prime}{\bf x}_{c;1}}{\bf e}\otimes\widehat{\mathscr{F}}_{12}({\bf x}_{c;1})\otimes\mathfrak{m}\bigl(\bigl(\widehat{\mathscr{F}}_{23}\circ\widehat{\mathscr{F}}_{12}\bigr)({\bf x}_{c;2}),z\bigr)&\\
\qquad{}+{\bf e}\otimes\hat{d}\bigl(\widehat{\mathscr{F}}_{12}({\bf x})\bigr)\otimes z&\text{if $k_{3}=0$}.\end{cases}

Note that in the case when
k_{3}\neq 0
the formula (
10.7
)
implies that the summand in the second line
vanishes unless
{\bf x}_{c;1}=1
.
In the case when
k_{3}\neq 0
and
{\bf x}_{c;1}=1
, it becomes
the sum in the third line.

In the case when
k_{3}=0
after a certain cancellation,
there remains another term, that is, the fifth line.

Remark 10.27
.

Note that
\deg^{\prime}{\bf e}=-1
. However, as we remarked in Remark

10.7
here the sign
\deg{\bf e}=0
is used.

On the other hand,

\displaystyle\hat{d}({\bf x}\otimes z\otimes{\bf w})={}

\displaystyle\hat{d}({\bf x})\otimes z\otimes{\bf w}+(-1)^{\deg^{\prime}{\bf x}+\deg z}{\bf x}\otimes z\otimes\hat{d}({\bf w})

\displaystyle+\sum_{c_{1},c_{2}}(-1)^{\deg^{\prime}{\bf x}_{c_{1};1}}{\bf x}_{c_{1};1}\otimes\mathfrak{m}\bigl(\bigl(\widehat{\mathscr{F}}_{23}\circ\widehat{\mathscr{F}}_{12}\bigr)({\bf x}_{c_{1};2}),z,{\bf w}_{c_{2};1}\bigr)\otimes{\bf w}_{c_{2};2}.

Therefore,
if
k_{3}\neq 0
, we have

\bigl({\mathscr{I}}_{12}\circ\hat{d}\bigr)({\bf x},z,{\bf w})=\sum_{c}(-1)^{\deg^{\prime}{\bf x}_{c_{1};1}}{\bf e}\otimes\widehat{\mathscr{F}}_{12}({\bf x}_{c;1})\otimes\mathfrak{m}\bigl(\bigl(\widehat{\mathscr{F}}_{23}\circ\widehat{\mathscr{F}}_{12}\bigr)({\bf x}_{c;2}),z,{\bf w}\bigr).

If
k_{3}=0
, we have

\displaystyle\bigl({\mathscr{I}}_{12}\circ\hat{d}\bigr)({\bf x},z)

\displaystyle\qquad=\sum_{c}(-1)^{\deg^{\prime}{\bf x}_{c_{1};1}}{\bf e}\otimes\widehat{\mathscr{F}}_{12}({\bf x}_{c;1})\otimes\mathfrak{m}\bigl(\bigl(\widehat{\mathscr{F}}_{23}\circ\widehat{\mathscr{F}}_{12}\bigr)({\bf x}_{c;2}),z\bigr)+{\bf e}\otimes\hat{d}({\mathscr{F}}_{12}({\bf x}))\otimes z.

Therefore,
{\mathscr{I}}_{12}
is a filtered
A_{\infty}
bi-module homomorphism.
∎

Lemma 10.28
.

(1)

The composition
\mathscr{I}_{21}\circ\mathscr{I}_{12,00}
is equal to the
identity.

(2)

The composition
\mathscr{I}_{12,00}\circ\mathscr{I}_{21}
is
chain homotopic to
the identity.

Proof.

(1) follows by an easy and straightforward calculation.
We will prove (2).
Let
x\in\mathscr{C}_{2}((\mathscr{F}_{12})_{\rm ob}(c_{1}),c_{2})
,

{\bf y}\in B\mathscr{C}_{2}[1](c_{2},c^{\prime}_{2})
,

z\in\mathscr{C}_{3}((\mathscr{F}_{23})_{\rm ob}(c^{\prime}_{2}),c_{3})
.
We observe

(\mathscr{I}_{12,00}\circ\mathscr{I}_{21})(x,{\bf y},z)=(-1)^{\deg x}{\bf e}\otimes\mathfrak{m}_{*}\bigl(\widehat{\mathfrak{F}}_{23}(x,{\bf y}),z\bigr).

We define

\mathfrak{H}(x,{\bf y},z):=(-1)^{\deg^{\prime}x}{\bf e}\otimes(x\otimes{\bf y})\otimes z
.

Let
\mathfrak{n}
be as in (
10.34
).
We calculate

\displaystyle(\mathfrak{n}\circ\mathfrak{H})(x,{\bf y},z)={}

\displaystyle(-1)^{\deg^{\prime}x}\mathfrak{n}({\bf e}\otimes(x\otimes{\bf y})\otimes z)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle x\otimes{\bf y}\otimes z+(-1)^{\deg^{\prime}x}\sum_{c}{\bf e}\otimes(\mathfrak{m}(x\otimes{\bf y}_{c;1})\otimes{\bf y}_{c;2})\otimes z

\displaystyle+{\bf e}\otimes\bigl(x\otimes\hat{d}{\bf y}\bigr)\otimes z

\displaystyle+\sum_{c}(-1)^{\deg^{\prime}{\bf y}_{c;1}}{\bf e}\otimes(x\otimes{\bf y}_{c;1})\otimes\mathfrak{m}\bigl(\widehat{\mathfrak{F}}_{23}({\bf y}_{c;2}),z\bigr)

\displaystyle-(-1)^{\deg x}{\bf e}\otimes\mathfrak{m}\bigl(\widehat{\mathfrak{F}}_{23}(x,{\bf y}),z\bigr).

On the other hand,

\displaystyle(\mathfrak{H}\circ\mathfrak{n})(x,{\bf y},z)={}

\displaystyle\sum_{c}(-1)^{\deg^{\prime}{\bf y}_{c;1}}\mathfrak{H}(\mathfrak{m}(x,{\bf y}_{c;1})\otimes{\bf y}_{c;2}\otimes z)

\displaystyle+(-1)^{\deg x}\mathfrak{H}\bigl(x,\hat{d}{\bf y},z\bigr)

\displaystyle+\sum_{c}(-1)^{\deg x+\deg^{\prime}{\bf y}_{c;1}}\mathfrak{H}(x,{\bf y}_{c;1},\mathfrak{m}({\bf y}_{c,2},z))

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\sum_{c}(-1)^{\deg^{\prime}x+1}{\bf e}\otimes(\mathfrak{m}(x\otimes{\bf y}_{c;1})\otimes{\bf y}_{c;2})\otimes z

\displaystyle-{\bf e}\otimes\bigl(x\otimes\hat{d}({\bf y})\bigr)\otimes z

\displaystyle+\sum_{c}(-1)^{\deg^{\prime}{\bf y}_{c;1}+1}{\bf e}\otimes(x\otimes{\bf y}_{c;1})\otimes\mathfrak{m}\bigl(\widehat{\mathfrak{F}}_{23}({\bf y}_{c;2}),z\bigr).

Therefore,

(\mathfrak{n}\circ\mathfrak{H}+\mathfrak{H}\circ\mathfrak{n})(x,{\bf y},z)=x\otimes{\bf y}\otimes z-(-1)^{\deg x}{\bf e}\otimes\mathfrak{m}\bigr(\widehat{\mathfrak{F}}_{23}(x,{\bf y}),z\bigl),

as required.
∎
